Kone to undergo fitness test ahead of Sassuolo clash

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Roma could recover a key player for their match against Sassuolo, scheduled for tomorrow at 8:45 PM at the Stadio Olimpico.

Gian Piero Gasperini hopes to be able to count on Manu Koné again, who has been sidelined in recent weeks with a muscle injury that has reduced the Giallorossi manager’s midfield options.

The Frenchman is completing his recovery, and a decision on his possible call-up will be made in the coming hours.

According to today’s edition of Il Messaggero, out now, Koné underwent personalized training yesterday, an important step in his return to training with the rest of the team.

The midfielder, who suffered a grade-one injury to his right hamstring during the match against Real Madrid, will undergo a trial today to assess his condition and determine whether he has the potential to return to the squad.

The immediate goal isn’t necessarily to start, but to be available to Gasperini again after his time out. In the meantime, the coach will be able to rely on Marten de Roon, Bryan Cristante, and Niccolò Pisilli to fill the two midfield spots.

Transformers Company Announces New Indie Publishing Label Focused On Toy-Like Games

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Toy company Takara Tomy, creator of the likes of Beyblade and Transformers, has this week announced an all-new indie game publishing label, the fifth such brand to be announced in the last year in a shake-up of Japanese gaming. Takara Tomy revealed the Enjoyn Games initiative alongside a trio of toy-themed titles, the first two of which will be released later this month.

According to Takara Tomy, Enjoyn Games aims to bring the companyâs experience making toys into guiding the development of new games. In that sense, its initial lineup has the feeling of more toy-like experiences, each emphasizing multiplayer. The first game, Stack and Sus, is a three-to-six-player social deduction game, with players divided into staff and spies, trying to make or sabotage a burger. Then there’s Sloppy Forgeries, a competitive party painting game tasking players with recreating classic art but with no ability to undo actions and all in a limited time, where accuracy determines a winner. Both of these games will release next week, on November 10 and 14 respectively. The third game, likely due in December, is Little Cheese Works, a puzzle-platformer for up to four players using voice chat to coordinate as mice negotiating levels to grab cheese, all without disturbing dastardly cats.

The crossover between gaming and toys for Takara Tomy seems obvious: each of these multiplayer experiences feels like a board game the company could have released, and there’s the potential to lean on the broader toy infrastructure in the company for promotion, and for future expansion to other mediums. It also, notably, makes Tomy the latest in a slew of major Japanese companies establishing transmedia initiatives that includes branching into gaming. Just before Tokyo Game Show, Sanrio announced Sanrio Games, with plans to release 10 games featuring Sanrio characters over the next three years. Toei Games has been set up to take the movie giantâs experience into indie publishing, also with a potential to broaden the IP if it’s successful. Then there’s PARCO Games, a division of the major Japanese department store Parco, which officially launched its label at the end of last summer.

Even beyond major studios, new publishers are embracing Japanese gamesâlast month Visions of Mana director Kenji Ozawa moved his Studio Sasanqua into the indie publishing space with its first game, Afantasia. This is on top of more established Japanese publishers like Playism and Shueisha Games, and the launch of a publishing label by Palworld developer Pocketpair. In almost all of these cases, the desire is not just to make money selling games, but to cultivate ideas that can be established in other mediums. The potential to find a game that could become something larger, as seen by The Exit 8, has been a driving goal for many. Whatever the motivations, Takara Tomyâs new game publishing label is a great endorsement of the Japanese indie gaming scene, as more companies and players see potential and excitement in the works of the country’s solo devs and smaller teams.

SCI-Arc task force champions long-term architectural strategies for fire-prone areas

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

the sci-arc resilient futures task force supports los angeles homeowners recovering from the 2025 wildfires, offering climate-resilient architectural solutions to replace outdated building habits.

The SCI-Arc Resilient Futures Task Force was created to rethink standard rebuilding methods after the January 2025 fires in Los Angeles exposed deep weaknesses in Southern California’s default residential. Bringing together teachers, researchers, and students, the initiative gives displaced homeowners direct, practical help with building safety, soil toxicity, emergency funding, and insurance claims through public community meetings.

Nearly two years later, residents are still rebuilding their lives as wildfires become more frequent and severe. Instead of repeating past mistakes caused by outdated safety codes, standard construction habits, and insurance gaps, this crisis presents a critical moment to create safer, long-term architectural designs in fire-prone areas.

Grounded in the institution’s tradition of critical design research, the Resilient Futures Task Force connects academic expertise directly with real-world disaster recovery. The coalition brings together an international network of architects, structural engineers, scientists, material specialists, non-profit developers, and financial strategists. Key collaborators and participating institutions include Code Red Consultants, Angel City Lumber, SGH, JPMorgan Chase, Century Housing, Clearinghouse CDFI, Builders FirstSource, Plant Prefab, Sexsmith Architects, Timberlab, ReSAWN, Urban Machine, and TU Delft.

Extending its focus from temporary aid to permanent resilience, the Task Force organized public panels on alternative building materials, mass timber construction, rebuilding economics, and fire-adapted landscaping to help communities make informed, long-term architectural decisions.

The initiative’s first year concluded with Architecture After the Fires, a landmark public exhibition showcasing nearly 70 speculative and actionable home design proposals tailored for fire zones. As the largest collection of Wildland-Urban Interface (WUI) architectural proposals to date, the showcase featured ongoing work from prominent practices including Lorcan O’Herlihy, SPF:architects, Studio Murnane, Morphosis, Heather Flood and Story Dimson Studio, Knowhow Shop, and Scott Uriu.

The Task Force is publishing a comprehensive book documenting these projects, creating a permanent reference guide for building in high-risk environments. Next, SCI-Arc plans to expand its research through institutional partnerships that combine artificial intelligence, environmental risk modeling, and advanced simulation to shape resilient building standards across the region.

school: SCI-Arc | @sciarc location: Los Angeles, California

Qantas Launches Historic New 15-Hour Nonstop Route From Sydney: See Map Now

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In an unexpected and highly intriguing network development, Qantas has announced a brand-new long-haul route. The oneworld member will begin flying from Sydney to the increasingly popular Cape Town.

It'll be historical in various ways. For example, it'll become Qantas' record third route to South Africa, while being the first time that Australia has had flights to Cape Town. It'll also be Cape Town's new longest route within the Southern Hemisphere. However, it will be far from Qantas' longest nonstop service on the Boeing 787.

On October 19, 2027, during the spring in the Southern Hemisphere, Qantas will lift off from its busiest and critical hub of Sydney Kingsford Smith Airport (SYD) to Cape Town International Airport (CPT). On a great circle basis, the brand-new route will cover 5,945 nautical miles (11,010 km) each way.

As this represents the shortest distance between two airports, real-world operations will increase that distance. There won't be any payload restriction, which means a full load of passengers and belly freight.

QF89: 10:15 AM-3:55 PM (same-day arrival; 14 hours and 40 minutes)

QF90: 5:55 PM-2:50 PM+1 (12 hours and 55 minutes)

Like so many new routes, SYD-CPT will be flown three times weekly. Given the block time and stage length of the airport pair, this means that one Boeing 787-9 will be able to operate it.

All of Qantas' 787-9s have 236 seats, which is not many. That's mainly because they're quite premium, with 42 seats in business (1-2-1), 28 in premium economy (2-3-2; 38" pitch), and 166 in economy (3-3-3; 32"). Around 18% of the capacity is in business.

Booking data shows that 87,000 round-trip passengers flew between Australia and CPT in 2025, with traffic up by nearly a fifth compared to 2024. While many South Africans live in Australia, it is unclear how many come from the CPT area. Many of the passengers would have been Australians visiting the increasingly popular city for tourism.

Unsurprisingly, SYD was the largest market to/from CPT. With around 23,000 passengers, the local traffic was not overly high, although the new nonstop flights may grow that by around 40%. The airline will also target passengers connecting to Auckland Airport (AKL), Brisbane, and Melbourne, in particular, with those places having around 42,000 passengers.

Significantly, SYD-CPT is relatively high-yielding, with the average fare across all airlines, cabins, and passengers being US$1,335 one way (excluding taxes but including a fuel surcharge). Reflecting higher premium demand, the average fare for SYD-CPT is around 12% higher than for SYD to Johannesburg's O. R. Tambo International Airport (JNB).

Interestingly, SYD-CPT flights will cover marginally less distance than SYD-JNB. As such, the fare difference is particularly notable, especially when coupled with similar operating expenses. Commenting on the new route, senior airline network planner, African specialist, and industry commentator Behramjee Ghadially said that:

"This new service will perform very well for Qantas, seeing how the Aussies love to visit new exotic places, especially where a combination of beach, wine and safari can be experienced in an upscale manner [although most safari-related activities are in eastern South Africa].

Qantas will also target tour operators selling South Africa packages from Australia and New Zealand as they can now offer an open-jaw itinerary for their clientele, such as flying into JNB and flying back from CPT using the same airline."

The carrier has just grown its footprint to this continent considerably. Find out more here!

As the above map shows, Qantas will now fly from SYD to CPT and JNB, along with from Perth International Airport (PER) to JNB. The latter route is the only one on which the airline has head-to-head competition, as it competes directly with the troubled South African Airways. It is also the one on which Qantas deploys an Airbus twinjet.

OAG data shows that Qantas flew between PER and JNB until 2023, with the route, which is nearly entirely overwater, returning 11 months ago, in December 2025. The operating A330-200 flies AKL-PER-JNB-PER-AKL. At some point, it then operates to SYD, where the specific airframe is switched.

Jackie Tohn Knows Getting Eliminated From <i><em>American Idol</em></i> Was a Gift

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The actress, who stars in Nobody Wants This, answers the “First, Now, Next” questionnaire

For the better part of four decades, Jackie Tohn has been steadily chipping away at Hollywood, building a career that has taken her from New York stages to the big and small screens. She has a particular kind of onscreen magnetism. As Esther, the scene-stealing sister-in-law to Adam Brody’s Rabbi Noah and Kristen Bell’s Joanne Williams in Nobody Wants This, she brings a particular kind of onscreen magnetism. Even at her most withering, she radiates the kind of energy that makes you want to be her best friend.

The New York native got her start onstage before landing early television roles in shows including The Nanny, Angel, and Veronica Mars. In 2017, a standout role in Netflix’s Emmy-winning series GLOW cemented her place in the industry. And did we mention she competed on Season 8 of American Idol? Out of about 150,000 hopefuls, she made it to the top 36. (The girl can sing.)

This year, Tohn has made headlines for reasons beyond her acting, opening up about learning she carries a BRCA1 gene mutation, which led her to undergo a preventative double mastectomy and, soon after, take up advocacy work. She’s been refreshingly candid about the experience. (Really, just ask her.) And she’s approached it the way she seems to approach everything in life: with grace, with wit, and with a laugh, because sometimes that’s just how you gotta roll.

A few weeks before the third season of Nobody Wants This dropped, Tohn sat down with Harper’s Bazaar to chat about sisterhood (and spitting in test tubes), an encounter with her idol, Joan Rivers (who fell asleep during Tohn’s performance in Jewtopia), and her Hollywood origin story (which includes Jessica Biel). Read on for her answers to our “First, Now, Next” questionnaire.

Cracked something open in me? There are so many that come to mind. My favorite movies in my early 20s were the ones that really cracked something open. Like Election and Rushmore and Wet Hot American Summer. Oh! And Waking Life, which was this movie that was rotoscoped, where they filmed it and drew over it. Those were all movies where I was figuring out who I was, and each of those movies really influenced me in a lot of ways.

My first true friend was Lisa Molina. Our mothers were very good friends. We met going on auditions because we were both theater kids, singing. My first celebrity friend was Ben Salisbury, and then my first celebrity best friend was Jessica Biel, who I lived with and is the reason I moved to L.A.

I came out to California in between semesters because we had this two-month break. My agent at the time, Aggie Gold, of the Fresh Faces Agency out of Baldwin, Long Island, famously said, “It’s pilot season, let’s see what we can get.” So me and my agent and my mom came out to L.A., and I was Ben Salisbury’s date to the TV Guide Awards in 1998, and I met Jessie Biel and never left. I’m 18 years old, and because my mom was with me, she got to meet Jessie’s parents, so she didn’t feel like she was releasing me to the wolves of living alone in Los Angeles. I lived in a gated community in Calabasas with Jess and her family. Kim Biel taught me how to write a check.

Honestly, I don’t know if there are better ones. Frankly, at the TV Guide Awards, the tables had polyester table skirts, and it was at a Marriott. It was delightful.

I’d been acting since I was nine, and I hadn’t really gotten the thing that was going to hit. When I was 23, I finally got a series. They fired everyone from the pilot, then it was picked up for six episodes mid-season, and I booked the series.

On our first work week, my parents came out, because it was a live studio audience. The next day, I was going to work and the producers called. They wanted me to come to their cabana at CBS Radford. My parents were in the car, so I go to drop them off at my dressing room, and someone was painting my name off my parking space. I was like, “Oh! Maybe they’re moving me.” I didn’t drop my parents off because I had no dressing room anymore; the door was locked. I drove them over to the cabana, and I was like, “What is going on?” And the first words out of their mouths were “Jackie, please calm down.” I’m like, “No, I’m not doing anything. My parents are in the car. This is mortifying.” That’s when they told me that the network wanted to go another way. And that the girl who was in the pilot, who I replaced, was now re-replacing me. I got fired. I made six episodes of network money. I had enough money at 23 to sort of be a person.

I got more into theater and more into music at that point because I was like, I can’t be at the behest of this business. I can’t be sitting here waiting for the phone to ring. I was playing a lot more music and doing a lot more theater. And that totally changed my life, because after this series I did a play called Jewtopia and moved to New York, and we did it off-Broadway for a few years. That’s when I started making records and really touring the country.

The second failure was getting eliminated from American Idol. That felt devastating, but it was the biggest gift. I didn’t make it to the top 24, and that’s where they would have really owned me.

Well, you never think it’s all going to be fine. As a person who started acting when she was nine and started getting real jobs at 36, those 27 years don’t go away when you finally get the thing you want. That said, the first time I had that feeling that I was part of something special, that mattered, was definitely the TV show GLOW. I remember being cognizant of it while it was happening. Me and Betty Gilpin would sit next to each other and be, like, “Just so you know, this is never going to happen to any of us ever again. This thing that we are doing right now and this sisterhood that we created and this female-powered camaraderie on this show, that’s tackling women’s issues, that’s written by, starring, directed by women? Executive produced, showrun, everything. This is a lightning-in-a-bottle situation.” You felt it when you were there.

I define career success as being financially stable in the career you want to have. There’s financial stability, but people are miserable. They have a lot of money, but they hate their job. For me, it was having enough money to get by in the arts and not have an additional 5–7 p.m. closet-cleaning, organizing job. When I was 16, it was to be a TV star. To be [Fran Fine in] The Nanny, specifically. The eye on the prize was, have that. Star in a half-hour multicam where I come down the stairs in various short sequined dresses.

Then, of course, the barometer by which I measure life success and personal success is so different. It’s having love in my life with my partner and my dogs, and having my health, having gone through what I have in this last year. Getting my genetic testing and doing whatever preventative surgeries I have to do.

The sisterhood is wild. I partnered with this genetic-testing company, Myriad. They discovered the BRCA mutation. I try to tell as many people as I can: Spit in a tube. It’s so easy.

I am my mother, so the answer to that is everything. The way we look, our facial expressions, the way we speak. I was that really rogue teenager who was always obsessed with my mom. I was hyper-aware that she was funny and smart and cool, and all the kids wanted to come to our house because my parents were the cool, lenient ones who always had the snack foods. I didn’t have a curfew because my mom was like, “Everyone else has a curfew. What are you going to do, stay out alone? You’ll come home as late as the last person comes home.”

My dad always played the piano, and I always sang with him. I could look back and be like, “Was that embarrassing?” No. I was ready; I was a showman at four. That was maybe a little annoying, when my mother would always want me to sing. But it wasn’t even that annoying. I wanted to.

My boyfriend and I were just in France and Italy over the summer. Both of our first times.

When I tell you it was grim until 36, now you believe. It’s crazy. Now that I’ve been, it’s even more unhinged. I bought Gucci bags in Florence, and they are proprietary to Gucci in Florence. They don’t go anywhere; they just stay there. I bought one for myself and one for my mom.

This is sort of a vague answer, but in general, I have trouble spending money on something I already have. If I’m out and I’m using a camera that has film, but I have three rolls of film in the fridge, it makes me insane to buy film because I have film. It used to be flying well, but it’s not anymore. Now that’s the number-one place it goes. Even hotels I don’t splurge on. When I’m doing comedy or speaking engagements around the country, I’m happy with a Hilton. I don’t need to be at a Ritz-Carlton for one night in the middle of Atlanta when I’m there for 18 hours. I’m this weird combination of … I wouldn’t say frugal, but I’m very mindful of how I spend my money. I am deeply not wasteful.

We are nothing alike! I’m just kidding. I know the biggest parallel and the biggest difference. The biggest parallel is that we are both absolutely strong, capable, opinionated Jewish women. I think something that differentiates us … I am a very, very foolish fool. It’s my whole thing. I love silly and whimsy. The sillier the better, the goofier the better. Talking in stupid voices. I’ve just been truly a clown my entire life, and Esther is not that way. She’s very straitlaced; she wants to be taken seriously. As I get older, it’s a little more important for me to feel like I’m being taken seriously, both as an actor and as a woman with opinions. But Esther’s not goofy like me. Esther would never be eating chips right now.

I’m saying yes to sisterhood. And no to people watching videos or FaceTiming on their phone in public without headphones.

Maybe I’d live on a ranch with a hundred dogs who need homes and need specialty care and are my very, very close friends who are dogs. I love dogs so much, I could fucking explode. It’s hard for me to walk down the street when I see dogs. Literally, I’m like, “How come that one’s not talking to me? How come that one is only with their own mom, and I get turned into a crazy person??

"More than an absence of colour": 4 artists reveal the keys to working in black and white

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The creative possibilities are endless if you know how to approach it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

With a whole spectrum of colours only a few clicks away, perhaps it’s not surprising that working in black and white is sometimes overlooked.

But whether you’re working monochromatically to draft an illustration or using it for a finished piece, a black and white approach is full of benefits. It can even make you stand out in an art world that's saturated with vibrant talent (if you need the tools for your own art, see our guides to the best drawing tablets and the best pens for artists).

Foregoing colour can be an intentional creative choice rather than the result of a limitation, but for illustrator Sarah Koudelka-Mason it was born out of convenience. “After trying out all sorts of tools, I found pencils to be the simplest, least fussy medium,” she says. “All you need is a pencil and paper, which you can bring anywhere. Perhaps because pencils were also my first and most consistent tool in my art journey, I feel the most comfortable with them.”

For character artist and illustrator Joe Vriens, a black and white approach was the result of a fear of colour theory mixed with a dash of impatience.

“I’d always get excited to see the bare line work of a comic, too; it seemed much more achievable,” he reveals. “I could grab a pen and paper and make a comic without expensive markers, paints or digital tools.

“I’m also old enough to remember King Kong in black and white. I made my parents rent that thing probably once a month. In retrospect, maybe King Kong informed my approach all along?”

Illustrator Isabella Mazzanti points out that working in black and white plays into a long tradition of art styles, including her own influences which range from Gustave Doré’s engravings to contemporary manga.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

“They seemed accessible and reproducible to me,” she says. “These early experiences were fundamental, because they shaped my aesthetic sensibility and my approach to drawing.”

Seeing as working in black in white is a deliberate approach, it also depends on specific considerations.

“You have to make sure the shading values are balanced well,” Sarah advises. “It’s easy for your shading to look flat or ’muddy’ if your tones are too similar, so you really have to figure out your darks, mediums and lights, and then tie them together with the between shading. I personally like to work lighter and work my way to the crisp dark lines at the end. So satisfying.”

“Line weight and shape design are also key,” says concept artist Alexander Joseba, who has worked for Epic Games, HBO, Ubisoft and more. “A strong silhouette can communicate a character or object very quickly, and different line weights can help separate forms and create depth. Since you cannot use colour to separate materials or planes, you need to do it through values, textures, edges and rhythm.”

“For me, black and white forces you to be more disciplined, although I also like to play with a more wild and instinctive approach. If the image works in black and white, it usually means that the composition, lighting and structure are solid.”

Alexander grew up looking at the work of great artists such as John Buscema, Mike Mignola, Alberto Breccia and many others.

“I was fascinated by the way they were able to communicate so much with ink: how they separated planes, created depth, controlled light and shadow, and made the whole image work so clearly, each one with their own very distinct style,” he explains

Some extra considerations are required when working in black and white, however. Alexander believes the most important consideration is readability.

“Without colour, you have to rely much more on values, contrast, shapes, silhouettes and composition to guide the viewer’s eye. Everything needs to be clear from the beginning: what the focal point is, what belongs to the foreground, middle ground and background, and where the main light is coming from.

“Working in black and white forces you to focus on the foundation of the image. You need to solve composition, lighting, values, silhouette, volume and overall readability properly. I think any artist who works well in black and white will also improve their colour work, because they understand values, light and structure better. Colour does not replace that foundation; it complements it.”

Isabella also warns against assuming that working in black and white is a simpler approach.

“A solid grasp of the visual language is essential in order to use it effectively,” she argues. “Black and white is not simply the absence of colour; when working with chiaroscuro, one chooses to tell a story based on duality, contrast and the exaggeration of ambiguity. I believe it is vitally important for an artist to understand this element, which has the power to intensify the viewer’s emotional engagement.”

When it comes to making black and white art work professionally, Isabella stresses that economic forces may be influencing publishers just as much as aesthetic considerations. “It lends images a classic, refined and timeless quality,” she says.

“Even when telling a certain type of story – for example, Gothic literature – black and white seems to be the most natural choice. It can be a risky choice, because one is foregoing the emotional appeal of colour, but when used in the right way and in the right context, it’s a winning decision.”

Sarah claims that black and white art is underrated, and that it’s not the usual approach commissioners are looking for. “But at the same time, if there are clients specifically looking for works of that nature and you’ve established yourself in that niche, you will find them. This applies to any unique niche of art, and I personally enjoy the challenge of going against the grain!”

For Alexander, black and white artwork is useful for both commercial production pipelines and for final illustration styles. “It depends on the project: sometimes it is a practical development tool, and sometimes it becomes the final artistic language,” he says.

Even if artists don’t gear their black and white artwork towards clients, focusing on it can still improve their overall craft. “Balancing tones in a final image is super-important, so if you can master greys then moving on to colour will feel easier,” says Joe.

“You can guide the viewer’s eye with tones alone, so it can help your compositions greatly. Focusing on greys or even black and white can take a lot of the pressure off as well, which allows you to really dig into lighting and form.”

Meanwhile Sarah is a firm believer that practising black and white art is one of the best ways to strengthen your understanding of the fundamentals.

“Without the distraction or support of vibrant colours, you are encouraged to look at your work in a simpler and more intentional way,” she says. “You start to see shape language, values, lighting, contrast, composition and depth a lot clearer.

Isabella agrees: “The absence of colour changes the way an image is perceived,” she says. “Rather than depriving the image of something, a black and white approach reveals its essential and most intimate nature; it speaks to the viewer in a more universal, almost archetypal language.”

Gigantic chasm on Mars is still tectonically active

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

New research shows that a powerful 2021 marsquake emanating from Valles Marineris — the largest canyon in the solar system — was triggered by unexpected movement in a fault line deep below the gigantic structure, revealing that tectonic activity can still occur on geologically "dead" worlds.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Mars' supersize Valles Marineris — the largest known canyon in the solar system — is still tectonically active even though the Red Planet is geologically "dead," new research on a previously record-breaking marsquake reveals.

On Aug. 25, 2021, NASA's InSight lander, which touched down on the Red Planet in 2018, detected a powerful marsquake, designated S0976a. The Martian equivalent of an earthquake reached magnitude 4.2, which, at the time, was the most powerful quake that InSight or any other Mars lander had ever seen. (Since then, a 4.7 magnitude marsquake in May 2022 knocked it off the top spot, but S0976a remains second on this list.)

Unlike most marsquakes, which have been minor tremors triggered by meteorite impacts near InSight, S0976a was a deep rumble that originated from the opposite side of Mars as the robotic seismologist, in the western half of Valles Marineris.

In the new study, published Oct. 15 in the journal Nature Communications, researchers reanalyzed the seismic wave data of S0976a and found that the quake originated from within a fault line running below the enormous canyon. The most likely explanation, they argue, is that there was tectonic movement in this hidden crustal crack.

"Valles Marineris is one of the most spectacular geological structures anywhere in the solar system, but until now we didn't know for certain that its faults are still active," study first author Jung-Hun Song, a researcher in the Department of Earth Science and Engineering at Imperial College London, said in a statement. "What we see is that this is not just a 'dead' fossil landscape, as faults deep beneath the canyon can still slip and generate marsquakes, long after the geological processes that originally formed them have ceased."

S0976a is the second-largest marsquake ever detected by NASA's InSight lander, shown here in a NASA clean room on April 30, 2015.

Valles Marineris is an enormous set of interconnected gullies and ravines stretching up to 2,500 miles (4,000 kilometers) in length, or roughly 10 times longer than the Grand Canyon. It is also up to 20 times wider and five times deeper, meaning that if you found yourself stranded at the bottom, it could take you a couple of months to climb out again.

The enormous chasm — named after the Mariner 9 spacecraft, which helped discover it in 1971 — is clearly visible from space, like a giant scar carved into the Red Planet's face. From orbit, you would also see the nearby Tharsis region, which is home to Mars' giant volcanoes, including Olympus Mons, which is at least three times taller than Mount Everest.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

These imposing structures are proof that Mars was once geologically active, perhaps even more so than Earth. However, scientists think the Red Planet's surface has remained relatively unchanged for nearly 3 billion years, according to the European Space Agency.

Valles Marineris is almost as long as the continental U.S. is wide. (In this photo, captured by NASA's Mars Odyssey orbiter, an optical illusion known as relief inversion may make the canyon appear as if it is raised above the surface, which it is not.)

The reason for this is twofold. First, Mars is smaller than Earth, so its core and mantle have cooled much faster than our planet's and are, therefore, less dynamic. Second, Mars has no tectonic plates. Instead of giant patches of crust butting against each other, the Red Planet's outermost layer is almost completely intact and acts like a giant lid over its cooling molten innards. (Research published earlier this week throws the "dead" theory into question, hinting that magma may still lurk below dormant Martian volcanoes).

Despite this, Mars still has fault lines. But rather than being a boundary between tectonic plates, these lines are more like giant cracks in its crust, some of which are visible at the surface.

In the new study, researchers discovered proof of what they call a slip-strike marsquake, where "blocks of crust move horizontally past one another along a steep, deeply rooted fault," the team said. They believe this occurred somewhere between 12 and 25 miles (20 to 40 km) below Valles Marineris.

The researchers also simulated several other potential causes of the marsquake, including a major asteroid strike or a landslide within the canyon, but nothing else could explain the patterns they saw.

Using seismic wave data captured by InSight, researchers were able to pinpoint the epicenter of the S0976a masquake (represented as a yellow star near the top of this map).

"What makes the result compelling is that several completely different types of evidence point in the same direction," Song said.

The finding also raises hopes that similar tectonic activity could one day be detected on other worlds across the solar system.

"Our study opens up exciting possibilities for discovering ongoing seismic activity on other planetary bodies that we might otherwise consider geologically inactive," Song said.

The discovery highlights the importance of detecting and studying marsquakes, which were only officially discovered in 2019, thanks to InSight. (Scientists have since uncovered older marsquakes, using data collected by the Viking landers in the late 1970s.)

InSight sent back a final selfie to Earth on Dec. 19, 2022, showing a heavy coating of Martian dust on its solar panels. Its mission was officially ended two days later.

"S0976a has turned out to be an extraordinary event," study co-author Doyeon Kim, head of Imperial College London's Terrestrial and Planetary Seismology Lab, said in the statement. "Its waves traveled through the core and helped us investigate the deepest part of Mars, and now they are telling us what happened at the source itself. From a single marsquake, we can learn both about the center of the planet and about an active fault beneath one of its most remarkable landscapes."

Scientists have similarly used marsquake vibrations from InSight to map out Mars' interior, leading to several intriguing subsurface discoveries, including dozens of blobs left over from ancient protoplanets, the hidden cause of the planet's uneven hemispheres, a potentially active "mantle plume" and a gigantic underground water reservoir containing enough liquid to cover Mars with a mile-deep (1.6 km) ocean.

Unfortunately, in December 2022, InSight quite literally bit the dust: A thick covering of Martian powder blocked its solar panels, preventing the lander from returning signals to Earth and thus ending its mission.

This means we currently have no way of detecting new marsquakes. However, the new study proves that the lander's archived data can still lead to exciting discoveries.

Song, J., Kim, D., Kolawole, F., Davis, J. M., Parry, R., & Maguire, R. (2026). Active crustal straining and fault reactivation at Valles Marineris. Nature Communications, 17(1). https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-78193-6

Mars quiz: Is your knowledge of the Red Planet out of this world?

Harry is a U.K.-based senior staff writer at Live Science. He studied marine biology at the University of Exeter before training to become a journalist. He covers a wide range of topics including space exploration, planetary science, space weather, climate change, animal behavior and paleontology. His recent work on the solar maximum won "best space submission" at the 2024 Aerospace Media Awards and was shortlisted in the "top scoop" category at the NCTJ Awards for Excellence in 2023. He also writes Live Science's weekly Earth from space series.

4-billion-year-old 'Rosetta Stone' found in Sahara Desert could unlock the secret history of water on Mars

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

A 4.1-billion-year-old meteorite discovered in the Sahara Desert offers a rare glimpse into Mars' early history.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A rose-colored rock discovered in the Sahara Desert may hold some clues to Mars' ancient geological history.

The meteorite, named Teghaza 001 after the salt plain in Mali where it was discovered in 2022, weighs roughly 1.8 pounds (800 grams) and formed more than 4.1 billion years ago. Blasted off Mars by an impact before eventually landing on Earth, it is one of the three oldest Martian meteorites ever found. The extraordinary age and unusual mineral composition of Teghaza 001 offer a rare glimpse into the formation of Mars' early crust and how volcanic materials may have been altered by water, according to a statement from NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California.

"It was only after we started looking closely that we realized this meteorite is really unique,” research leader Yang Liu of NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California said in the statement. "One of the paper’s reviewers called it a Rosetta Stone for Mars."

Unlike many Martian meteorites, Teghaza 001 contains a distinctive combination of igneous minerals and textures that preserve evidence of its complex history. Its mineral composition resembles materials observed in Mars' southern highlands by NASA's Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter.

Although researchers can’t pinpoint exactly where the meteorite originated on Mars, they have identified 13 potential locations on the planet where it may have formed.

"This is really our first piece of Martian crust from a part of Mars we’ve never had a sample of before," team member Lee Saper, NASA’s Ames Research Center in Silicon Valley, said. "It records interactions of water and rock and maybe even an imprint of the Martian atmosphere as it was losing water."The meteorite was initially identified as Martian based on its oxygen isotopes. To determine its age, scientists then examined tiny zircon crystals embedded in the rock. Using a specialized rubidium-strontium dating technique, researchers analyzed individual mineral grains on its surface, preserving it for future analysis.

The results revealed that Teghaza 001 was altered by water around 4.1 billion years ago, suggesting the rock itself formed even earlier. The mineral and chemical evidence points to a dynamic water cycle on ancient Mars, when liquid water interacted with the planet's crust and potentially left traces of its atmosphere as it gradually lost water.

Scientists believe further analysis of Teghaza 001 could reveal more about how water shaped the Martian surface and influenced the planet's evolution. The study also marks the first time researchers have used rubidium-strontium dating to analyze an ancient Martian meteorite, opening the door for the technique to be applied to other samples in the future.

"We've only scratched the surface," Liu concluded.

Their findings were published on Thursday (Oct. 29) in the journal Science. A separate study examining the meteorite's water content was published on June 10 in the journal Science Advances, offering additional insight into early Mars.

Previously, her work has been published in Nature World News. When not writing or reading about science, Samantha enjoys traveling to new places and taking photos! UK ministers seek powers to investigate when AI agents are let off the leash

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Cyber Security and Resilience Bill could pave way for searches, seizures, and inspections when operators lose control of automated systems

The UK government is paving the way for rules requiring essential service operators and digital providers to guard against losing control of AI agents and report incidents when safeguards fail.

An amendment to the Cyber Security and Resilience Bill, which is passing through Parliament, would give ministers powers to regulate the deployment and reporting of agentic AI in certain organizations. The new clause was introduced during the bill's third reading in the House of Lords this week.

In an explanatory note, ministers outlined plans for safeguards, notification of affected organizations, preservation of evidence, and government-led incident reviews. The measures would require consultation and parliamentary approval through secondary legislation.

The move follows a series of incidents. Last month, OpenAI revealed that its agents repeatedly strayed beyond their intended scope, prompting the company to notify more than 100 organizations about the issues. That came after July's incident, in which an OpenAI agent accessed the internet and breached machine learning platform Hugging Face. The episode, and the potential for more damage from poorly designed AI agent deployments, have prompted MPs to question whether UK law can deal with such threats.

Speaking to The Register, Stuart Davey, a partner at law firm Pinsent Masons, described the clause as "quite novel." Rather than directly imposing new duties, it would allow ministers to introduce rules and investigatory powers covering regulated organizations. Davey said that could include powers "to look where there has been such an AI agentic compromise or issue, and do some things... appoint inspectors, enter and search premises, seize and retain documents." Under the proposals, the minister could confer certain powers on regulators, requiring action from targeted organizations.

Potentially affected organizations include gas and electricity suppliers, managed service providers, and cloud providers deploying AI agents for customers. Foundation model developers would not automatically fall within scope simply because they build the underlying technology.

"Where there's a security or operational compromise in relation to a relevant network or information system, that would seem to capture those managed service providers or cloud computing providers who are using AI, and there's that loss of control," said Davey. He advised cloud and managed service providers to track whether the proposed measures become law.

Evin McMullen, CEO and co-founder of AI identity and control infrastructure company AmanTrue, said the amendment recognizes loss of oversight or control as a security risk but does not itself impose new duties.

"The amendment allows future regulations to introduce investigatory and enforcement powers where an incident involves an AI agent, targeting organizations running essential digital services," he said.

"The deployer of an AI agent chooses its permissions, connects it to systems, and gives it access to data. When something goes wrong, the deployer is the party that will be expected to explain what happened.

"Accountability stays with the organization that put the agent into the system, and ultimately with executives and the board. Whether model builders are captured will depend on how the regulations are written and whether a builder sits in a regulated category or a regulated supply chain." ®

Met Gala’s New Theme Is So Basic

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Brancusi at MoMA, trouble at Pace, a rediscovered Georgia O'Keeffe painting, and The Met presents the biggest rescission indicator yet.

Jackson Pollock at The Met, Roy Lichtenstein at the Whitney, and Constantin Brancusi at the Museum of Modern Art — our critic Aruna D'Souza is tired of this dead-White-men fest. That said, she still has a soft spot for Brancusi. She went to see the Romanian artist’s solo show at MoMA, and she has some thoughts.

Speaking of dead White blokes, visitors to the MFA Boston will soon be able to watch a live conservation of Dutch painter Nicolaes Eliasz Pickenoy’s “The Six Founders of the Amsterdam Wine Guild” (1625). Watching paint dry never sounded so appealing.

In living-White-men news, Pace Gallery, owned by father-and-son duo Arne and Marc Glimcher, has reported steep losses. Meanwhile, a recently rediscovered Georgia O'Keeffe painting is heading to auction and might sell for millions.

But if that’s all too exciting, get a load of the Met Gala's new “everyday” theme.

Let’s get one thing out of the way first: For all my weariness about how canonical dead White men are STILL. CONSISTENTLY. CENTERED in museum exhibitions — even after said museums have publicly taken pains to expand collections, hire curators with broad expertise and interests, and invite new audiences into their spaces — I have ZERO complaints about putting on a Constantin Brancusi exhibition. Even in fall 2026, when the city’s three major museums have turned themselves over to Brancusi, Lichtenstein, and Pollock simultaneously. (I will go to my grave insisting that it should have been a Krasner show.) That said, the work of this particular canonical dead White man is glorious, and continues to resonate with contemporary artists: His name has come up consistently in interviews with sculptors this past year, whether Rodney McMillian or Martin Puryear or a number of others I’ve talked to. Brancusi: The Artist and His Studio at the Museum of Modern Art is a great reminder of why.| Aruna D’Souza Read More

This Fashion Scion’s Laid-Back Ibiza Wedding Featured Lace Dresses, Barefoot Vows, and Dancing Till Dawn

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It took the world grinding to a halt for Lola Teller, 29, and Freddie Woodruff, 35, to get going. A mutual friend had set them up on a date in 2020, but with Lola studying in Edinburgh and Freddie building his catering business, Noco, in London, geography got in the way. That is, until 12 months later, when lockdown found them separated by little more than a field in Boscastle, Cornwall, where Lola’s mother, fashion photographer and stylist Venetia Scott, owns a house, and Freddie was setting up a restaurant. “He used to come and cook for us every night, and a few weeks in, my mum was like, ‘Look, why don’t you come and live with us here?’” Lola says. “She was very fond of him, and I think she liked the food, so she was pushing.” What followed was an idyllic few months spent picking mussels on the beach and taking boats out into the Celtic Sea to catch lobsters. Eventually, though, it was time for Lola to begin her career in the art world—she is now director of the agenda-setting Bernheim gallery—and the couple moved in together in leafy north-west London. This time for good.

It was in July 2025 that Lola and Freddie got engaged, while staying at her family home on Hydra, where she has spent summers since childhood. “Fred decided he wanted to go on a hike at 4 p.m. in the boiling heat, and I was like, ‘This is ridiculous, no way,’” she says. “Something must be going on.” With nerves and temperatures running high, they barely spoke on the way up, and Freddie almost lost the ring—a trio of east-to-west oval Sophie Breitmeyer diamonds—among the rocks. “We got to the top of the mountain, completely dehydrated, just as the sun was setting, when he told me to sit on a rock and got down on one knee,” she recalls. “And it was really quite beautiful.” From there, the would-be-weds jetted off to Es Cucons, a family-run hotel in northern Ibiza, where the owners, who had been welcoming Lola and her mother for years, offered to host their wedding. “They said, ‘We don’t usually do this, but how does 40 guests sound?’, and we were like, ‘...150?’” They settled on 90, returning to the hotel at Christmas, Easter, and once more the following summer to finalize the plans.

There were even more visits—10 in total—to Kensington-based bridal designer Phillipa Lepley to perfect the lace wedding dress, complete with corset, veil, and train, and designed with the input of her mom. Having a mother behind some of Vogue’s most memorable covers must have come in handy? “Throughout the process, she was making me try on these enormous poofs of dresses, which looked great but were never gonna be the one for me,” Lola says. “Often I’d be like, ‘I like this!’, and then mum would be like, ‘...No.’ She’s always right, though.”

On the day, she paired the dress with white silk platform heels from “not a nice brand,” and diamond-and-pearl drop earrings borrowed from her stepmother’s collection. She would walk down the aisle flanked by her mom and dad, Juergen Teller, though despite having two of fashion’s most directional photographers at her fingertips, she still hired traditional wedding photographers to document the occasion. “Well, you want some of the classic shots,” she explains. “Plus, they wanted to enjoy themselves.” Indeed, Lola says Teller didn’t take “a single photo” throughout the weekend.

Which began with guests gathering for welcome cocktails among the fig and almond trees of the farmhouse hotel, before Lola, Freddie, and their closest family members headed to Chiringuito Cala Xuclar for an intimate dinner of grilled fish on the beach. “I think I had an hour of sleep that night,” Lola says, “which might have been because we were drinking quite late, but also… the nerves.” The morning of the wedding began with Lola kicking Freddie out of their room, before setting off on an hour-long coastal walk with her mum and bridesmaids. Saunas and ice baths followed, as they took their time getting ready for the ceremony. Once laced into her dress, Lola made her way up the hillside to a secluded grove, where her Armani-clad groom was waiting. Flanked by her parents, she walked down the aisle to Mazzy Star’s “Fade Into You,” though her entrance didn’t go entirely to plan. “It’s such a slow song, so it was really hard to time,” she says. “My veil got caught on a stone and nearly ripped my head off, which made it quite comical.” Following behind was a giddy procession of flower girls in La Coquetta dresses, among them her three-year-old sister, all walking at their own pace. “My 12-year-old goddaughter was sending me dresses that were, like, £500 each. Nice try.”

Lola’s uncle officiated the ceremony, which included a poem written by Freddie’s mother, a group singalong to Bob Marley’s “One Love,” and Lola tossing her bouquet as the newlyweds made their way back down the aisle. “I was ready for a drink at that point,” she says. “It was time.” The couple joined their loved ones in the hotel gardens for canapés of padrón peppers, octopus skewers, croquettes, and jamón, along with speeches and photographs, before everyone was ushered into a candlelit pergola for dinner, waving their napkins in the air to The Shapeshifters’ “Lola’s Theme.”

Dinner was served family-style: lamb chops, roast potatoes, and Spanish salads, before Lola steeled herself to make a speech of her own. “I was petrified, about to burst into tears,” she says. “But after a few drinks, it was fine, and I’m so glad I did it.” The room then erupted into an impromptu rendition of John Denver’s “Take Me Home, Country Roads,” giving Lola a chance to slip away and change into a beaded Clio Peppiatt minidress.

Back at the tables, a giant slab of tiramisu was wheeled in for the newlyweds to serve to their guests, who were in no hurry to move on. “I was like, ‘Guys, they’ve literally converted the yoga tent into a dance floor.’” DJ Fiona Jane played Ibiza bangers until 12:30 a.m., before the party spilled out around the swimming pool. “They were up till seven—the sign of a good wedding,” Lola says. “Freddie and I were asleep, luckily.”

The following morning was a slow one, with guests resurfacing in the afternoon for enormous pans of poolside paella—under mercifully cloudy skies, after days spent seeking refuge from the sun beneath parasols and behind fans—as friends of the couple took turns behind the decks. Lola wore a lace beach dress by Away That Day over a white bikini, before changing into a crochet Reformation number for one final hurrah at legendary nightlife institution Pikes. Well, the last official hurrah, anyway.

A handful of guests extended their stay, dropping in on the newlyweds at Atzaró Agroturismo Hotel—a 13-hectare farm-to-fork property where the tinkling of goats’ bells drifts over from neighboring farms and chickens roam freely—for a few days of decompression before Lola returned to London for the annual art-world frenzy that is Frieze. “A lot of people say that they come back and they have loads of wedding blues, but I just didn’t; I was just so happy that everything went so perfectly,” she says. “The whole thing was surreal, just the best feeling, heaven. I’ve spent every day since looking at the photos and trying to relive the happiest memories with our favorite people.”

Dancing into dinner to “Lola’s Theme” by The Shapeshifters.

“11/10” Tuna Melt and More Recipes We Made This Week

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s no secret that Bon Appétit editors cook a lot for work. So it should come as no surprise that we cook a lot during our off hours too. Here are the recipes we’re whipping up this month to get dinner on the table, entertain our friends, satisfy a sweet tooth, use up leftovers, and everything in between. For even more staff favorites, click here.

My Test Kitchen buddy Rebecca Firkser has a wild little pasta up her sleeve that I can’t get enough of: Butternut Pasta With Sausage and Dates. When she first made it for the team, I was blown away. I would have never thought to throw dates into a pasta, but alas, it’s delicious. Each bite has something sweet, something creamy, a bit of crunch, and endless fun. —Jesse Szewczyk, senior Test Kitchen editor

Since my son started daycare in September, our household has been sick more than it has been healthy. So it makes sense that I have been on a chicken soup kick. I have it for lunch and dinner and drink mugfulls of stock between meals. My latest batch was my colleague Rebecca Firkser’s Toasted Orzo Chicken Soup, which has a beautifully spiced broth and lots of nourishing spinach. It may not be a cure-all, but close enough. —Emma Laperruque, cooking director

Delicata squash should be as popular as butternut. It’s far easier to cut (no hacks or hacking necessary), the thin skin is not only edible but tasty, and it’s got a sweet potato-like flesh that’s creamy and, well, sweet. I usually treat it simply (oil, salt and pepper, hot oven, done), but I treated myself to my former colleague Rachel Gurjar’s gussied-up Roasted Delicata Squash With Crispy Onions and I’m so glad I did. The squash itself takes no extra effort, but gathering up some toppings, like store-bought fried onions tossed with extra spices, juicy pomegranate seeds, and seasoned yogurt, transformed it into a dish I was proud to serve to guests. —Kelsey Jane Youngman, senior cooking editor

We have a new Friday tradition at our house: Pasta Alla Vodka and Caesar salad. Not rocket science, I know, but after a rough-and-tumble work- and school-week, we are all reaching for some semblance of comfort. It is also easy enough to whip up that I can often beat the timing of our former Friday tradition, takeout pizza. The Caesar dressing is usually made in advance (I keep a batch in the fridge because my family lurves it) and the pasta of choice is usually rigatoni. It cooks in about the same amount of time as the sauce—I just love when that happens. —Hana Asbrink, deputy food editor

There’s nothing more perfect than a tuna melt. Buttery slices of sourdough or rye that leave a shine of grease on your fingers. Tuna salad mashed into tender flakes, speckled with crisp celery, onion, and herbs. A blanket of American cheese, so stretchy it’ll rival a mozzarella stick. And a mandatory pickle, to offer added tangy solace after each bite. I ate mine with salt-and-vinegar kettle chips stuffed inside because that’s how my favorite diner does it. —Nina Moskowitz, associate editor, cooking

BA Bake Club Has Your Holiday Showstopper Covered

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Lucky you: We’re sharing December’s BA Bake Club drop early so you can get started on holiday planning right…now!

For members of Bon Appétit Bake Club, every month is filled with special treats, but the holiday season calls for something bigger, grander, even more spectacular. This Triple Chocolate Mousse Cake ticks every box. And it’s just enough of a challenge to ensure you feel accomplished. The one-bowl chocolate cake is dense and deeply fudgy; the mousse is airy and light; and the thin slip of ganache on top brings it all together. No matter what kind of year you’ve had, this dessert is the way to cap it all off. Here’s how to make it:

This mousse has three foundational pastry techniques hidden in plain sight. It starts with a crème anglaise, a lusciously pourable custard. Adding that to a bowl of chopped chocolate yields a crémeux, a velvety and dense emulsified custard. Folding in whipped cream finally turns it into a fluffy mousse. Magical.

It’s important to use a 70% chocolate here. It’s intense enough to shine through the heavy cream and has enough cocoa butter to provide structure and stability, ensuring the mousse doesn’t slump.

A springform pan is a worthy and necessary investment, ensuring an anxiety-free presentation. It also allows you to bake and build the cake in just one tin.

For an architectural slice that looks like it strutted out of a fancy patisserie, dip the blade of your knife in hot water, then wipe it clean with a dry kitchen towel between each cut.

Triple Chocolate Mousse Cake

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

This chocolate mousse cake ticks a lot of boxes—big, grand, ideal for the holidays, and just enough of a challenge to ensure you feel accomplished. The one-bowl chocolate cake layer is dense and deeply fudgy; the mousse is airy and light; and the thin slip of ganache on top brings it all together. No matter what kind of year you’ve had, this dessert is the way to cap it all off.

Making the mousse alone will teach you three foundational pastry techniques, and they’re all hidden in plain sight. It starts with a crème anglaise; adding that to chopped chocolate yields a crémeux, and folding in whipped cream turns it into a mousse. Magical. The cake can be easily made from start to finish in the space of a morning, but can be split up further to fit your schedule. Bake the chocolate cake layer and chop all the chocolate on the first day; make the mousse and assemble the cake the following.

Welcome to Bon Appétit Bake Club, a community of curious bakers. Each month senior Test Kitchen editors Jesse Szewczyk and Shilpa Uskokovic share a must-make recipe and dive deep on why it works. Come bake and learn with us—ask questions, share pics, listen to our podcast, and lots more here. And don't forget to join the Bake Club Group chat over on Substack.

1 hour 30 minutes (plus 6 hours for chilling)

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 350°. Lay a sheet of parchment paper over base of pan. Fit band on top and clamp shut to hold parchment in place; scrunch up around base. Wrap a sheet of foil around bottom and up sides of pan (this will help prevent any leaking).

Whisk together 1 cup (125 g) all-purpose flour, 1 cup (200 g) sugar, ⅓ cup (28 g) Dutch-process cocoa powder, 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and ½ tsp. baking soda in a medium bowl. Add 1 large egg, ⅓ cup buttermilk, ⅓ cup vegetable oil, 1 tsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract, and ½ cup water and whisk until smooth, about 1 minute. Scrape batter into prepared pan.

Bake cake until a tester inserted into the center comes out with a few moist crumbs attached and top springs back when gently pressed, 25–35 minutes. Transfer pan to a wire rack and let cake cool in pan 10 minutes. Remove band from base; slide cake off parchment and base and onto rack. Let cool completely; discard parchment and foil.

Do Ahead: Cake can be baked 1 day ahead. Store tightly wrapped at room temperature.

Place 10.5 oz. bittersweet chocolate, preferably 70% cacao, coarsely chopped, in a large heatproof bowl. Bring 1 cup whole milk to a simmer in a small saucepan over medium, about 5 minutes.

Meanwhile, vigorously whisk 4 large egg yolks and ½ cup (100 g) sugar in a small heatproof bowl until smooth and lemon yellow, about 1 minute.

Gradually stream hot milk into yolk mixture, whisking constantly until combined. Return to pan, scraping bowl clean with a heatproof rubber spatula. Cook over medium, stirring constantly, until thickened just enough to coat a spoon, about 5 minutes. (Do not let custard come to a boil or it will curdle.) Immediately pour over chocolate; let sit 1 minute. Add 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt and 1 tsp. vanilla bean paste or vanilla extract and whisk vigorously until chocolate is melted and crémeux is smooth.

Beat 1¾ cups heavy cream in the bowl of a stand mixer fitted with the whisk attachment on medium speed until soft peaks form, about 5 minutes. Add one quarter of whipped cream to crémeux; mix until combined (this will lighten it). Add remaining whipped cream in 3 additions, gently folding after each until incorporated before adding more.

Reassemble springform pan; line bottom with a parchment paper round. Wrap a strip of parchment around inside of pan to create a collar that stands about 1" above band (ends should overlap by at least 3"). Using a large serrated knife, trim off mound on top of cake to create a flat surface if needed. Return cake to pan. Spoon mousse over and smooth into an even layer with an offset spatula or spoon. Place pan on a rimmed baking sheet; chill until top of mousse feels set, about 2 hours.

Place 4 oz. bittersweet chocolate, preferably 70% cacao, coarsely chopped, in a medium heatproof bowl. Bring ½ cup heavy cream and ¼ tsp. Diamond Crystal or Morton kosher salt to a bare simmer in a small saucepan over medium. Pour over chocolate and let sit 2 minutes. Whisk gently until smooth. If ganache feels hot, let cool until lukewarm but still pourable, 8–10 minutes.

Pour ganache over mousse and smooth with offset spatula. Chill until mousse is completely set (ganache will set in about 30 minutes), at least 4 hours and up to 12 hours (no need to cover unless fridge has other strong smells).

To serve, remove band from base and peel parchment strip away from sides of cake. Transfer cake with base to a plate and let sit 20 minutes; sprinkle top with flaky sea salt.

Why TLC's Whitney Way Thore Began Taking Weight Loss Medication

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As Whitney Way Thore revealed in the upcoming season of My Big Fat Fabulous Life, she began taking weight loss medications. The TLC star explained to E! News why she decided to give them a shot.

Years into her journey to welcome a baby, Whitney Way Thore is ready to give just about anything a shot.

And so when her fertility doctor suggested the My Big Fat Fabulous Life star consider using a weight loss medication, which can have an impact on ovulation and reproductive hormones, she listened.

"She said, 'Just take it, and then if you get pregnant, stop,'" Way Thore shared with E! News in an exclusive interview. "And so that's why I started it."

Ignoring any particularly loud outsider opinions, "I didn't really have any side effects or anything like that," detailed the 42-year-old, back with the 10th season of her TLC series (returning Nov. 10 at 10 p.m.). "I think physically it was easy."

But committing to a prescription was just a small piece of Way Thore's fertility journey. The bigger piece involved an admittedly terrifying conversation with now-fiancé Jake Beaudoin.

Though the pair are engaged now, they were dating more casually when Way Thore told him about her dreams of getting pregnant.

"I was obviously very nervous to tell him that that was my plan," said Way Thore who had found a sperm donor in the hopes of getting pregnant through intrauterine insemination. Though, she added, "I hadn't really expected that it would matter to him because I didn't expect that we would be in a serious relationship."

And she assumed any hope of that would be dashed when she filled him in on the details.

"I thought, 'Well, I'm going to tell him this and obviously, and understandably, you're not going to stay with me through this,'" Way Thore shared of her line of thought. Instead, she marveled, "He was so supportive and so excited, and he never wavered on that once and still hasn't. So, that was really amazing."

Which is an apt descriptor for their entire relationship.

Having been through the romantic wringer—"Obviously if you're still single at this age, you've been through some s--t," Way Thore reasoned—she had her eye out for any red flags the DJ may be throwing in the air.

"I've almost never dated a man that I leave the relationship having any shred of respect for," stressed Way Thore. "When you're this old and you've dated as much as I have, and been as disappointed in men as I have," she detailed, well, it can be hard to see the good in people.

"You want to protect yourself and you want to not be as stupid as you might have been," she explained. "Or, you might want to approach things in a smart way."

However, she acknowledged, "I think there's a balance between doing that and being hyper vigilant because I think that kind of hyper vigilance can definitely stop a relationship before it has a chance to really get off the ground."

Admittedly, the North Carolinian waved a scarlet-tinged banner when he ghosted Way Thore after they initially connected in 2018.

"Basically we met, we only hung out a couple times," she explained. Still, "I bring it up constantly. He, for the longest time, would try to defend it, and then it became this thing, and I just got so annoyed."

But when it comes to Beaudoin that's certainly not her dominant emotion.

"When you go from thinking you're just in a casual relationship to all of a sudden we're going to try to have a baby together, things move very quickly," Way Thore explained. "And that can expose a lot of weaknesses that you might feel. But I continue to be pleasantly surprised at every turn, even though I was really, really, really looking for reasons to believe that it wasn't right."

Basically, acknowledged Thore, she's grateful "he proved me wrong."

And, yes, she's pretty psyched to be rocking an aquamarine gemstone on her left hand. "I think when you're in love, you naturally want to share that," she explained of letting viewers into her engagement journey. She joins a long list of celebrities work their way down the aisle.

Can a Radar-Equipped AC Actually Cut Your Power Bill?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

With rolling heat waves sweeping the globe, 2027 is shaping up to be the hottest year on record. As the health risks of extreme temperatures mount, there's never been a better time to rethink sustainable cooling.

Air conditioners and electric fans account for around 20 percent of electricity used in buildings worldwide—3 to 4 percent of total greenhouse gas emissions—and, as demand for cooling in homes, workplaces, and schools grows, the number of AC units in use could reach 5.5 billion by 2050.

The solution isn't one magic fix, but a suite of emerging technologies heading for living rooms and apartment windows. From radar sensors tracking your body to compact heat pumps and climate-safe refrigerants, here is how the next generation of air-conditioning plans to keep us cool without cooking the planet.

In 1994, Mitsubishi Electric was zooming past the air-con competition, introducing the first motion and occupancy sensors for AC, with infrared 3D sensing eventually following in the mid-2010s. Fast-forward to 2026 and AI-powered ACs now track people in the home with millimeter-level precision.

AC makers have launched intelligent, targeted air conditioner units all year, from Samsung’s Bespoke AI system in March to Dyson’s Find+Follow feature for its Purifier Cool fan in June. At IFA in September, we got a whole sweep from Chinese companies like Hisense, Dreo, and Midea.

LG’s latest DualCool AI AC has a 5-meter radar sensor for people detection plus targeting based on your everyday movement patterns. Hisense’s Companion Living AC also uses data from smart home sensors to identify and precool “target paths” within the home, based on your routines. So if you always head from the couch to the kitchen for a midnight snack, your AC can cool that particular zone before you even get up.

The real-time cooling features aim to improve comfort inside sticky homes. A secondary benefit is that precisely locating people and delivering cooling directly to them could reduce energy consumption. “It definitely has the potential to reduce the total energy use for air-conditioning,” says Björn Palm, senior professor in energy technology at the KTH Royal Institute of Technology in Stockholm. By how much? That's less clear.

Dyson’s Find+Follow purifier and fan uses a simple, low-resolution camera, feeding the “stick figure”-like, anonymous coordinates of its 17-point person detection into an algorithm, all instantly processed on-device and deleted, without any face recognition. “It can tell that you’re a human, whether you’re sitting or standing, which direction you’re moving," says Alex Hudson, an environmental care engineer at Dyson. "It might map your arms and shoulders.”

The Purifier Cool can oscillate 350 degrees between “up to six people” in the space, directing airflow for personalized cooling. One hour after it detects that everyone has left the room, the appliance will switch to Auto mode and—if there is no big pollution spike for the purifier to deal with, like a truck driving past an open window—it’s a "considerably lower power draw than when it’s in fan mode, so there’s definitely a benefit there,” Hudson says.

Samsung’s Bespoke AI WindFree Pro adds a motion radar sensor, which can recognize and track two occupants. “AI Direct Wind” can follow and blast you for immediate cooling, whereas “AI Indirect Wind,” you guessed it, cools the spot you’re in without blowing air right in your face. Samsung’s residential environment tests claim energy savings of “up to 30 percent” with its overall AI energy mode switched on. At IFA, Samsung added wearable data: a Galaxy Watch or Galaxy Ring can signal to the AC when you’ve fallen asleep, triggering a gentler Windfree Cooling mode overnight.

Midea’s VitaBreeze AC, launching in early 2027, combines user photos with millimeter-wave radar sensors to personalize cooling. During setup, the user manually uploads a series of room images to the app, indicating the air conditioner placement. The AC then uses those images with radar data to create a 3D virtual grid of the room and furniture position.

Millimeter wave (or mmWave) radar—the kind used in 5G cellular technology—bounces short-wavelength radio waves, typically 1 to 10 millimeters, to detect an object's distance and angle, as well as tiny movements in real time. Here, the Midea app’s Follow Me feature, similar to Samsung's, can pinpoint your real-time position in the room, triggering louver adjustments as you move around. Manuel Seethaler, head of Midea's Residential Air Conditioning product strategy in Europe, says that its technology can save more than 30 percent of energy in cooling mode compared with units without these algorithms.

The average AC sold in 2024 was already about 20 percent more efficient than an equivalent model in 2014. However, there’s still plenty of room to grow, as the IEA predicts that “cooling demand” will grow by 1,600 terawatt-hours by 2035—Japan and South Korea’s total electricity combined. But this kind of proximity sensing could also result in marginal energy savings.

“There is a performance improvement you can get if [targeted AC] is done well,” says Vincent Romanin, founder and CTO of San Francisco–based Gradient, which makes 120-volt all-weather window AC/heat pumps. “But we’re going into buildings where we’re reducing the energy by 85 percent and to design another feature that gets us to 87 percent? I personally have never used a product with an occupancy sensor that I feel like worked well.”

For Palm, to reach sustainable home cooling by 2050, we need three major changes: cheaper, compact heat pumps for all home sizes; fewer toxic natural refrigerants (fluids and chemical compounds that absorb and release heat); and newly designed systems with low charge to ensure safety.

Heat pumps have become a modern, efficient option for heating and cooling. For heating, it's a four-stage process: absorbing outside heat to produce gas via a refrigerant; compressing to raise the temperature; condensing, where heat is passed to the water in your home’s heating system; and cooling back into a liquid via an expansion valve to drop the pressure. When cooling is needed, the reversing valve switches back to run the process from inside to outside for air-conditioning.

The key is to persuade renters and people living in smaller homes to make the overall switch. “For the domestic market, we need heat pumps which fit into an apartment without being too noisy or bulky,” Palm says. It's also critical to reduce the price.

Gradient units typically use one-third less energy than a standard window AC, mostly for heating but with cooling efficiencies too. They’re a quick self-install option that lets apartment renters get their window back, see lower bills, and run a quieter AC system than older models.

But fixing the form factor only solves part of the equation. Palm’s other criteria—replacing toxic synthetic refrigerants with natural alternatives like propane (R290), as in Bosch's Climate 6000iP, which launched in September—present a tougher engineering challenge. Leaking refrigerants alone account for an estimated 720 million metric tons of CO2-equivalent emissions every year. Natural refrigerants dramatically curb the potential for global warming, but their flammability demands complex, spark-free redesigns and low-charge systems that US regulators and supply chains are still cautious about embracing.

Reaching sustainable cooling by 2050 won’t be glamorous, Palm says. Ultimately, the future of home cooling won't hinge on an air conditioner that tracks you to the kitchen for a midnight snack—it depends on the unsexy, invisible mechanical work happening deep inside the compressor.
