This may be the best evidence yet that dinosaur blood can fossilize

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Minerals may have preserved remnants of ancient blood in a dinosaur bone, but some are skeptical

Using a technique called scanning electron microscopy to peer inside an 115 million-year-old dinosaur limb bone, researchers captured images of tiny ellipsoid structures, about 20 micrometers in diameter, that may be fossilized red blood cells. Molecular analyses found preserved heme — the oxygen-carrying component of hemoglobin — in the bone.

The life and times of a dinosaur may be written in its blood — if blood can be preserved in bone.

Tiny microstructures found in the limb bones of a fossilized 115-million-year-old specimen are the remnants of red blood cells, researchers report November 2 in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. The team says these are both the oldest red blood cell structures yet identified and the first to be chemically proven. But not everyone is as sanguine about that conclusion.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

The ability to identify such structures could offer a powerful molecular window into the physiology of long-extinct animals, including their metabolism and even activity levels, says paleontologist Kliti Grice of Curtin University in Bentley, Australia.

The bone, from a fossil-rich site in northeastern Brazil called Mina Pedra Branca, belonged to the dinosaur Aratasaurus museunacionali. Grice and her colleagues used a variety of chemical and imaging analyses to study the Cretaceous Period specimen.

Maurilio Oliveira/Wikimedia Commons (CC BY 4.0)

The team prepared thin cross-sectional slices of bone and imaged details of the limb bone’s internal structure with focused electron beams. That revealed ellipse-shaped microstructures within the limb’s fossilized network of blood vessels.

A map of the ellipsoids’ chemical element composition revealed that they were full of iron, carbon and nitrogen, all components of a biomolecule called heme. Heme is an iron-bearing, ring-shaped molecule found in hemoglobin, the protein in red blood cells that carries oxygen throughout the body.

Then, the researchers used a technique that identifies molecules by how they scatter laser light. Those analyses suggested the presence of porphyrins, ringlike organic compounds found in blood — and the precursors of heme. Finally, they ruled out contamination from other porphyrins, such as the pigment chlorophyll, by separating and identifying the molecular contents of a bone extract. That experiment also suggested heme.

Taken together, these findings point unequivocally to the preservation of molecular traces of ancient blood, the team says. They also offer a clue to a novel pathway that might preserve those traces for so many millions of years.

Blood cells decompose within days of the death of an organism. Only unusual and fast-acting environmental and biochemical conditions could have stopped decay and held those tissues in place as the organism fossilizes.

In this fossil, the secret ingredient was a manganese oxide mineral called hausmannite, the researchers say. The team’s analyses had found the mineral within the ellipsoid structures. That mineral, they propose, may have acted as a time capsule, preserving the cellular structures and protecting the molecules within, including collagen amino acids.

Distinguishing fossilized blood cells from nonorganic minerals that formed within a fossil after the organism’s death is a tricky task. During fossilization, mineral-rich fluids seep in and around the bones, and the minerals that form within the fossil can bear a strong resemblance to bodily structures.

In a 1997 study, two paleontologists analyzed tiny spheres found inside the limb of a fossil, possibly a pterosaur. The spheres were once thought to be the fossilized remnants of ancient blood cells, but the duo determined that the spheres were instead pyrite, an iron mineral that had been deposited in the bones after death.

Those paleontologists suggested that, going forward, several criteria should be met before proposing a blood cell origin for fossilized structures, including comparing the shape of the structures with that of living related organisms and identifying certain biomarkers in the structures. One of those biomarkers is heme compounds.

Grice and her colleagues say that their study meets these criteria. Unlike the pyrite, hausmannite was crucial to preservation, “rather than simply a mineral that replaced biological material after it had disappeared,” she says. The close spatial relationship between the hausmannite, the blood cell-like structures and the chemical signatures of heme “raises the possibility that mineralization helped stabilize, entomb and protect components of the original biological material during fossilization.”

But Mary Schweitzer, a paleontologist at North Carolina State University in Raleigh who was not involved in the new study, says she doesn’t think these findings do fully meet those criteria.

The heme biomarkers, for example, don’t appear to be localized to the round structures alone, as would be expected in fossilized blood cells, but come from analysis of the entire bone. The proposed red blood cells also differ in size and shape compared with red blood cells from living birds and reptiles, she says.

Schweitzer is no stranger to skepticism over the discovery of ephemeral tissues in fossils. In 2005, she and her colleagues reported the first recovery of flexible, soft-tissue blood vessels from the 68-million-year-old fossilized leg bone of a Tyrannosaurus rex. It was a landmark study that sparked debate, including over how it would even be possible to preserve such tissues through deep time, as well as the most surefire way to detect them in the present.

“I am not convinced” that the research team has definitively identified heme compounds, Schweitzer says. “I do applaud them for bringing a plethora of methods to the study. Extraordinary claims require extraordinary evidence, and that does require state-of-the-art technology.”

No one really knows what a dinosaur’s red blood cell shape would be, let alone how the process of fossilization might distort the original shape of the structures, or allow heme-related molecules to escape their cellular confines over time, Grice says.

And, she says, her team’s interpretation of the evidence doesn’t just rely on the observations of those structures. “[It] rests on the convergence of anatomy, morphology, mineralogy, elemental analysis and molecular chemistry,” she says. “That combination is critical.”

As for whether other fossils might have molecular traces that have been preserved by hausmannite, “we simply don’t know yet,” Grice says. “But this type of preservation may not be quite as rare as we currently think.”

Questions or comments on this article? E-mail us at feedback@sciencenews.org | Reprints FAQ

K. Grice et al. Heme signatures in mineralized red blood cells within vascular canals of a 115-million-year-old dinosaur bone. Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. 2026. doi: 10.1073/pnas.2614248123

D.M. Martill and D.M. Unwin. Small spheres in fossil bones: Blood corpuscles or diagenetic products? Palaeontology. 1997.

Carolyn Gramling is the earth & climate writer. She has bachelor’s degrees in geology and European history and a Ph.D. in marine geochemistry from MIT and the Woods Hole Oceanographic Institution.

The Cabinetmakers’ Autumn Exhibition features a rug-wrapped chair and a giant candle holder

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The 2026 edition of the Danish furniture show has opened at Museum Ordrupgaard, with works by 36 designers on show alongside historic paintings and sculptures.

For this year’s edition of the Cabinetmakers’ Autumn Exhibition, or Snedkernes Efterårsudstilling, designers were invited to contribute works that explore the relationship between light and form.

The theme was chosen to reflect this year’s venue, the Snøhetta-designed galleries of Museum Ordrupgaard, which house important works of French and Danish art from the 19th and 20th centuries.

“In both impressionism and furniture design, light becomes a means of shaping an atmosphere and creating a sense of presence,” the exhibition overview stated.

“While the painters explored the impact of light on colours and atmosphere, designers examine the encounter between light and form, materials and surfaces, as use, function and aesthetic fuse into one, and even minor details and variations in light influence our perception of the furniture.”

The results include a 1.8-metre-high floor lamp that looks like a candle holder, by designers Karen Kjærgaard, Nanna Gram and Peter Skrzipczyk, and an elm wood chair wrapped in a patterned wool rug, by designers Karin Carlander and Christian Street.

Other eye-catching works include a cube made out of squashed ceramic vessels, by ceramicist duo Claydies, and a heavy bronze piece designed to look like a lightweight woven chair by designer Hans Sandgren Jakobsen.

The Cabinetmakers’ Autumn Exhibition has been an annual fixture since 1981, but its origins go back even further.

It began as a revival of the historic Cabinetmakers’ Guild Exhibition, which ran from 1927 to 1966 and debuted some of Denmark’s most iconic furniture designs, from Børge Mogensen’s Spanish Chair to Hans J Wegner‘s Round Chair.

Most of the works are contributed by members of the association, which is made up of over 80 designers, architects and manufacturers, although a handful of guests are invited to join each year.

One of this year’s most attention-grabbing works was contributed by guest exhibitor Matteo Fogale, a Uruguayan designer based in Copenhagen.

Fogale partnered with lighting brand Astep on a family of curvaceous ash wood lamps of different heights, topped by rotating discs that double as both lights and dimmer switches.

August Ostrow’s Soft Structure Lamp is another of the guest works. The American designer took cues from folded metal forms found in heating and ventilation machinery to create his copper and paper table light.

Timber craft is well represented in the show, with one particularly impressive piece by designer Andreas Lund and cabinetmaker Søren Risvang Snedkeri.

The pair collaborated on a table with an experimental frame of slender pine rods and 3D-printed joints.

Anna Søgaard designed a pair of cherry wood frames with opposing curves, while Claus Landry Bjerre crafted a pine wood cabinet incorporating 3D-milled replicas of 3D-scanned hazel branches.

Notable works in other materials include Carlo Volf’s wire-frame lamp, Zeyu Rong’s curvy translucent screen, and a light formed of four triangular glass tubes by Jonas Trampedach and Viola Heyn-Johnsen.

The Cabinetmakers’ Autumn Exhibition is on show at Museum Ordrupgaard in Charlottenlund from 23 October to 13 December 2026. See Dezeen Events Guide for more design events around the world.

How 4 states voted on election-related ballot measures

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

More than 100 statewide ballot measures went before voters this week, including a handful that could shape future elections in their states.

Here’s a look at how those measures fared, including one in Alaska that hasn’t been called as yet:

Voters in Maryland on Tuesday approved an amendment to the state’s constitution that will make it easier for the state’s Democrat-controlled legislature to redraw the congressional map for the 2028 elections.

The outcome was a victory for Democrats, who could potentially eliminate the state’s lone Republican-held seat – the 1st District on Maryland’s Eastern Shore, represented by Rep. Andy Harris, the chair of hard-line conservative House Freedom Caucus.

The Old Line State emerged as a bright spot in Democrats’ plan to counter the redistricting wars initiated by President Donald Trump and turbocharged by the spring Supreme Court ruling that diluted Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act and set off a slew of Republican-led map-remaking across the South.

But those GOP-led redistricting efforts appeared to backfire this week, with Democrats holding many of the redrawn seats in states such as Texas, Florida, Alabama and North Carolina.

Still, Maryland Gov. Wes Moore told reporters Wednesday that he was “ready to engage” with the legislature on a new map, citing the threat of further redistricting in Republican states.

“Donald Trump is not done. He has already been very clear about his intention to go after additional states, to go after places like Mississippi and South Carolina and Indiana,” Moore said, according to video posted by WBAL-TV 11 News.

Following an unsuccessful attempt to redraw the Maryland map in February, Moore called a special August legislative session during which state lawmakers voted to put the redistricting amendment, known as Question 3, on the November ballot.

The move faced immediate legal challenges from Republicans. Two Maryland courts ruled to block the measure from appearing on the ballot, finding that lawmakers had missed the legally imposed deadline to refer the ballot measure. The state’s Supreme Court, though, allowed the redistricting amendment to go before the voters, with changes to the ballot text.

State and national Democrats campaigned heavily for Question 3 as some public polling pointed to skepticism among voters. Former President Barack Obama even appeared in an ad urging voters to approve it.

Voters in the Show-Me State overwhelmingly rejected a Republican-drawn congressional map that sought to dismantle a safely Democratic seat in the Kansas City area.

Missouri Republicans enacted the new lines in 2025, aiming to give them an advantage in seven of the state’s eight House districts, including the 5th District held by Democrat Emanuel Cleaver II.

A protracted legal and political tussle over the map ensued.

The group People Not Politicians Missouri started a citizen petition to put the adoption of the map to a statewide vote. While the petition effort was pending, the state held its August primaries under the new lines. But in September, the state Supreme Court ruled that the 2025 map could not be used in the general election because of the pending ballot measure and ordered state officials to hold November’s election under the previous boundaries, first enacted in 2022.

Attempts to challenge the ruling in federal court ultimately proved unsuccessful, with the U.S. Supreme Court issuing an order that reinstated the 2022 map.

Groups on both sides of the ballot measure, known as Proposition A, reportedly spent millions to persuade voters. But St. Louis Public Radio reported last week that while opponents of the 2025 map were actively campaigning against it, organized efforts in favor of Proposition A appeared “nearly nonexistent.”

Republican efforts to try again and enact a new congressional map for 2028 could be complicated by a constitutional amendment, approved by voters Tuesday, that curtails the power of state legislators to override the result of a voter-approved initiative.

Voters in Massachusetts opted this week not to join states such as California and Washington and adopt a nonpartisan primary system.

The ballot initiative, known as Question 3, would have replaced the state’s partisan primaries with an open primary under which all candidates would appear on the same ballot, with the top two vote-getters, regardless of party, advancing to the general election.

The outcome was a victory for the chorus of labor unions and progressive groups, including the Working Families Party, that opposed the measure. Holding so-called jungle primaries, the groups argued, could make it harder for candidates without financial resources or high name recognition to run for office.

A bipartisan coalition that included the NAACP of New England and Republican former Gov. Charlie Baker supported the measure, arguing that open primaries would give voters more choice and create more competitive elections in a state dominated by Democrats.

Alaskans weighed in this year for the second time as many cycles on a ballot measure to revert to partisan primaries and repeal the state’s top-four ranked choice voting system.

With over 70 percent of the vote in, The Associated Press had yet to call the ballot initiative, known as Measure 2, which would also roll back certain campaign finance disclosure requirements. As of Thursday evening, the “no” side, which supports keeping the current election system, was narrowly ahead. The state has until Nov. 18 to finish counting outstanding ballots.

Under Alaska’s voting system, all candidates ran on the same ballot during the primary. Then the top four candidates, regardless of party, advance to the general election, which is decided by ranked choice voting if no one takes a majority of the vote.

In 2024, Alaska voters narrowly voted to keep the current system in place.

National Republicans, though, have largely opposed ranked choice voting, including President Donald Trump, who has characterized Alaska’s voting system as a “scam.”

The state’s use of open primaries and the top-four ranked choice system has drawn fresh scrutiny this year.  Outside groups spent big sums trying to boost underdog candidates during the Senate primary in an effort to hurt the front-runners. And several candidates in the Senate, House and governor’s races dropped out before the deadline to finalize the general election ballot, again trying to prevent splitting votes with their party front-runners.

Then last week, the Senate race was upended by a New York Times investigation that revealed operatives aligned with the Senate campaign of Democratic Mary Peltola helped recruit a candidate with a similar name to her opponent, Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan in an attempt to stack the four-candidate ballot with two Republicans with the same first and last names. The revelations have fueled an ongoing debate there about whether ranked choice voting encourages this kind of campaign chicanery.

“We would not be having this conversation at all if we didn’t have a thing in place called ranked choice voting,” Mike Dunleavy, Alaska’s outgoing Republican governor, told Fox News last week.

Dunleavy has ordered an investigation into the revelations, as has the Justice Department. The DOJ also sought to block the certification of the state’s Senate results if the incumbent Sullivan loses. Last weekend, a federal court dismissed the lawsuit.

Alaska and Maine are the only two states that currently use ranked choice voting in regular federal elections, along with the District of Columbia.

Michael Macagnone contributed to this report.

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.5M in Crypto From 35 Investigations

A former FBI agent has pleaded guilty to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency using wallet credentials the bureau had collected during its own investigations, the Justice Department said on Thursday.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, pleaded guilty to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled to be sentenced on March 4, 2027.

Former FBI agent Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, pled guilty today to wire fraud and money laundering related to using his access to steal cryptocurrency.@FBIWFO https://t.co/jDVtSiAM3H

— U.S. Attorney EDVA (@EDVAnews) November 5, 2026

Yaroch worked at the FBI's Boston Division from 2017 to early 2025 on a squad investigating an unnamed "adversarial nation state," before moving to the bureau's Counterintelligence and Espionage Division, where he had access to FBI systems holding crypto credentials gathered in investigations, according to prosecutors.

Beginning no later than December 2024, he used that access to steal crypto from wallets controlled by subjects of other investigations, by nation-state actors, and from at least one wallet the FBI had created for an operation. He made about 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials from 35 separate FBI investigations, then swapped the stolen funds into other cryptocurrencies and moved them through further transactions to hide their origin. He used some of the money to pay off credit cards, car payments and other loans.

Yaroch also planned a new life abroad. He asked ChatGPT how to maximize profits from investments and where to live in the European Union, and after the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers power of attorney to obtain a tax identification number for him, prosecutors said. He arranged to travel to Portugal in September.

His actions compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, as well as sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection, according to the plea agreement's statement of facts.

Yaroch turned himself in. On July 29, he broke down while telling a former colleague at the Justice Department's National Security Division that he had made "very poor decisions" involving crypto wallets, according to an FBI affidavit filed in the case. He then self-reported to FBI headquarters, saying he had become frustrated that the bureau "could not or would not act" against an adversary's crypto accounts and had decided to "take matters into his own hands."

At the time, Yaroch said he had made about 10 to 12 transfers worth under $1 million, far less than the figures in his plea. Agents searching his home two days later seized a Trezor hardware wallet, three passports and a slip of paper bearing seed phrases, according to the affidavit. His Kraken account held about $188,600, mostly in U.S. dollars and the USDC stablecoin, with smaller amounts in Squid (QUID) and Bitcoin. A further $933,800 sat in Suilend, a lending protocol on the Sui blockchain, which he had accessed through the Slush wallet app. Yaroch told agents he chose Suilend "simply because he liked that the logo was a water droplet."

Agents transferred about $925,000 from those accounts to government-controlled wallets, the affidavit said. The roughly $165,600 held as U.S. dollars on Kraken could not be moved the same way.

Decrypt reported on the charges against Yaroch in August.

Singapore Airlines Unveils New First & Business Class Cabins For A350, 777-9

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The wait is finally over — Singapore Airlines has today unveiled its all-new cabins, which were first teased two years ago. Most significantly, this includes a brand new business class, plus the expansion of first class to more planes. This really represents the carrier’s next generation product offering, since these cabins will be installed on future Boeing 777-9s, as well as retrofitted on dozens of Airbus A350s.

Let’s first talk briefly about which planes will be getting this product, and then we’ll cover the details of the cabins. Keep in mind these changes will also include an updated soft product, including a new dining concept as well as new amenities across cabins.

Singapore Airlines’ new cabin products will be coming to all of the carrier’s upcoming Boeing 777-9s, as well as to select Airbus A350-900s. This includes a new first class, business class, premium economy, and economy, along with updated inflight entertainment and the installation of Starlink Wi-Fi.

Singapore Airlines has 31 Boeing 777-9s on order, which are expected to eventually be the carrier’s flagship aircraft. These will come delivered with the new cabins. As a matter of fact, Singapore Airlines had initially planned to debut the new cabins on these 777-9s all the way back in 2020. However, delivery of those planes has now been pushed back to late 2027 at the earliest.

More immediately, Singapore Airlines also plans to invest 1.1 billion SGD (~835 million USD) to update the interiors of its entire long haul Airbus A350-900 fleet. This consists of a total of 41 jets, and includes the carrier’s seven A350-900ULRs, with the “ULR” standing for “ultra long range,” as these planes operate the world’s longest flights.

The only A350-900s that won’t be reconfigured are the ones that are specifically designed for regional flights, featuring the same cabin products you’ll find onboard Boeing 787-10s.

How will the layouts of A350s change, once reconfigured? Singapore Airlines’ standard long haul A350-900s:

Singapore Airlines’ ultra long range A350-900s:

With the above out of the way, let’s take a look at Singapore Airlines’ new first class and business class seats. Let me say upfront that:

Below is a chart with the basics of Singapore Airlines’ new first class product.

Singapore Airlines’ new first class seat is designed by PriestmanGoode, and manufactured by Collins Aerospace, and it’ll be available exclusively on Boeing 777-9s and Airbus A350-900ULRS (so not on standard A350-900s, and it won’t be retrofitted on existing aircraft).

I’d describe this product as being a pretty traditional first class product in a 1-2-1 configuration, with large seats that turn into beds that are 80″ long and 30″ wide, each with a privacy door. Each first class seat also supports face-to-face dining, with a large table that provides ample space for two people to enjoy a meal together.

Entertainment will be available on a 32″ 4K monitor. As you’d expect, tech will be cutting edge beyond that, with bluetooth audio, wireless, AC, and USB-C charging, etc. There will also be a touchscreen handset that provides easy access to KrisWorld, plus a panel with seat controls and flight information.

One of the nice things is that the center suites have a retractable divider, potentially creating a large, expansive space, so that guests in the center seats can dine and relax together. Singapore Airlines claims that when combined, the double bed becomes the biggest bed in commercial aviation, as it measures 80″ long and 60″ wide.

There won’t be overhead bins in first class, so bags can be stored in a cabin baggage compartment underneath the seat. There will also be a personal wardrobe, and a leather-lined vanity drawer.

What’s my take on Singapore Airlines’ new first class? Quite honestly, this is almost exactly what I was expecting from the airline:

Below is a chart with the basics of Singapore Airlines’ new business class product.

Singapore Airlines’ new business class seat is designed by Designworks, and manufactured by RECARO, and it’ll be available exclusively on Boeing 777-9s and Airbus A350-900s (both long haul and ultra long haul).

This business class product will be in a staggered 1-2-1 configuration, with seats that turn into beds that are 79″ long and 24″ wide, each with a privacy door.

Entertainment will be available on a 20-22″ 4K monitor. As you’d expect, tech will be cutting edge beyond that, with bluetooth audio, wireless, AC, and USB-C charging, etc. The airline also highlights how the seat has multiple storage options, which is appreciated.

Perhaps what’s most interesting is how in every other row, the center seats will be very close together, with the ability to form a double bed, which is a feature that customers always appreciate.

What’s my take on Singapore Airlines’ new business class? In some ways it’s what I expected, but there are some real surprises here:

Maybe it’ll look different in person, but am I the only one who is genuinely confused by the color choices? Those yellow accents just feel very… Spirit Airlines or Scoot, no?

When can we expect Singapore Airlines’ new premium products to debut? The expectation is that we’ll see a long haul Airbus A350-900 reconfigured first, and the first of those planes should now enter service in the second quarter of 2027. We don’t yet have any details beyond that.

The expectation is that the first retrofitted A350-900ULR (the ultra long range plane, operating the world’s longest flight) will debut at some point later in 2027, though that’s still up in the air. Meanwhile the 777-9s should start to join the fleet in late 2027 or early 2028 — only time will tell, given never-ending delays with Boeing.

Singapore Airlines should finish retrofitting its 41 A350s with the new cabins by either 2030 or 2031, though that timeline also remains subject to change. The delays with these new products reflects industry-wide supply chain constraints, as well as delays in the certification of the new seats (a common industry problem). The aircraft will be retrofitted by SIA Engineering Company in Singapore.

Which routes are likely to get the new cabins first? While we’ll see how this plays out, based on historical precedent, it’s likely that Sydney (SYD) and London (LHR) will be the first two destinations, since those are the first destinations where the airline has sent all of its new long haul products.

Singapore Airlines has just unveiled all-new cabins, which includes a new first class and business class. These cabins will be retrofitted on select Airbus A350-900s, and installed on all factory fresh Boeing 777-9s. These new products are long overdue, and I can’t wait to see them in service.

In first class, Singapore Airlines opted for a product that feels on brand, with warm colors, and a functional design that even allows for a double suite in the center seats. Meanwhile in business class, the airline chose a staggered product that looks solid, but I just can’t get over the cabin finishes… maybe it’s just me.

What do you make of Singapore Airlines’ new long haul cabins?

Are Your Gains Stalling? Here’s Why Your Training Needs to Keep Evolving

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

Some guys have training logs so old they ought to be registered as historic relics. Monday is chest, Tuesday is back, and somewhere in there are three sets of ten with the same dumbbells they’ve been using since the Clinton administration. They sweat, they grunt, they look in the mirror, and they wonder why nothing much changes.

Two studies published this month have given that old problem another going-over. Both looked at progressive overload, the practice of increasing resistance as you adapt to training. Not exactly a revolutionary concept. Six-hundred years ago, Milo of Croton slung a calf over his shoulder and walked the hills behind his farm every day until the calf became a bull. Guess what happened to Milo? He grew. Six-hundred years later Joe Weider called it his progressive overload system. The new research doesn’t prove the legend. It does suggest that the basic idea is still worth considering.

While moving more weight leads to more gains, here's why doing it properly matters most.

The first study, published October 14 in the Journal of Strength and Conditioning Research, involved 41 previously untrained young women. Researchers assigned their arms to different conditions and had them perform one-arm curls three times a week for eight weeks. One training condition added weight when the participants reached the top of an eight-to-12-repetition range. Another kept the weight and repetitions fixed. There was also a nontraining comparison condition. Both exercising conditions built muscle, but the progressive group gained more thickness in the elbow flexors.

Notice what that doesn’t say. It doesn’t say the women who stayed with the same weight grew nothing. They did grow. A beginner can make progress on a stimulus that would put an experienced lifter to sleep. And the researchers weren’t comparing entire bodybuilding programs, just a tightly controlled exercise. If somebody tells you this proves you must add five pounds to every lift every Tuesday, he’s selling certainty the study doesn’t contain.

A second paper, published October 15 in the International Journal of Sports Medicine, put 31 untrained adults through twelve weeks of leg extensions. Each person trained one leg with increasing loads and the other without increasing the load, twice weekly. Both legs gained muscle. Across the full study, the groups weren’t significantly different at the individual measurement points. But during the final three weeks, growth was greater in the progressively loaded legs. The researchers also found that the fixed-load legs ended up working further from failure. In other words, what had once been hard became relatively easy.

Aha! That’s worth dragging back into the gym. Progressive overload isn’t a religious requirement to put more iron on the bar at every workout. It’s a way of preventing yesterday’s challenge from becoming today’s warm-up. You can progress by adding repetitions, improving control, increasing useful range of motion, or eventually increasing weight. The particular method depends on the exercise, the person, and what they’re trying to accomplish. Keeping a record helps because memory has a remarkable talent for turning a mediocre set into a personal record by the time you get home.

There’s another wrinkle. A separate study in The Journal of Physiology, published at the end of 2025, found similar muscle growth with heavier and lighter loads when sets were taken to voluntary fatigue. Is that a contradiction? The question there was how heavy the weight had to be when effort was comparable. These October studies asked what happens when the weight is allowed to progress or is deliberately held back. Load, effort, and progression are related, but they aren’t interchangeable.

I wouldn’t build a national training policy around two small studies of beginners doing curls and leg extensions. They don’t tell us exactly how a seasoned bodybuilder should manage a twenty-year training career, and they certainly don’t justify grinding every set into the floor. Joints, recovery, and the occasional week when life kicks your ass still count.

But they do make a decent case against autopilot. If you’re getting stronger, doing more quality work, or making the same weight harder through better execution, you’re giving your muscles a reason to pay attention. If you’re doing the identical thing indefinitely and expecting a different result, don’t blame your genetics, your supplements, or start thinking your drugs are fake. The weights aren’t holding you back. Your imagination is.

Nottingham Forest’s historic goal drought at home is part of longstanding problem

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Oliver Glasner’s side are yet to score at City Ground this season but troubles in front of goal precede his arrival

Nottingham Forest and Schalke are yet to score at home in 2026-27. The former are one of only five teams in the history of the English top flight to have failed to score in their first four home league matches. Three of the preceding four also drew a blank in game five, which will be deflating news for Oliver Glasner with Manchester City heading to the City Ground on Saturday looking for a win after consecutive league defeats. Forest’s expected goals total implies they should have scored but a flaw in their data shows a reason why.

They also failed to find the net in a Carabao Cup home game with Leeds, meaning they have amassed 70 shots in all competitions without giving the home fans a goal to celebrate. As extreme as this situation has become, Forest scored only two goals more than the lowest home scorers in the Premier League last season (Burnley), drawing a blank on nine occasions. This is a longstanding issue.

The dishonour of scoring the fewest home goals in Europe’s big five leagues in modern times belongs to Dijon, who bagged eight in 19 Ligue 1 games in 2020-21. Fulham went one goal better in the same season to claim the Premier League’s wooden spoon for home scoring. Both scored three across their first four games, showing the unlikely depths to which Forest’s attack has sunk at the City Ground.

With expected goals a frequent element of post-match analysis, it can be easy to overlook that shots are independent of each other. One is not more likely to be converted when those that preceded it were missed, even though the team’s xG total ticks upward. A side that produced one expected goal through 100 shots valued at 0.01 xG would be far from guaranteed to score.

This reality lies at the heart of Forest’s home woes. Their 62 league shots have been worth 3.58 expected goals. It may feel reasonable to assume they deserve three goals. But only two of the efforts were deemed by Opta to be big chances, only five more had a better than one-in-10 probability of being converted and more than two-thirds were valued at 0.06 xG or less. Only against Coventry did they muster at least one expected goal in total, needing 24 attempts to register 1.09 xG.

Forest’s chance quality is so poor because many of their efforts are struck from long range. They have taken 50.3% of their City Ground shots from outside the box, at least 6% more than any Premier League team did on their respective patch in any of the past five seasons.

Glasner’s side are having to do this because they are struggling to get the ball into the penalty area. Again, this is a problem that predates the Austrian’s appointment. Forest were below the league average of 36.6% for their home pass accuracy into the box in 2025-26 (33.3%) and have sunk lower this season (29.6%). Cross accuracy is not the most instructive metric because the most dangerous deliveries can evade teammates if they are not anticipating them. Nonetheless, Forest’s home data paints the same picture as with their passing; below league average (26.6%) for completion rate last season (21.7%) and worse again in 2026-27 (19.0%).

Having to shoot from distance has helped make them chronically unable to put shots on target. Forest have done this only nine times in the league at the City Ground, giving them a shot accuracy rate of 14.5%. It is unsurprising to see Schalke on 18.8% given they are equally goal-shy. The least accurate home side across the past 10 seasons of football in Europe’s big five leagues are the Parma class of 2025-26. Even they managed to put 23.7% of their shots on target when taking last place in a sample of 974 team season examples.

If Forest don’t score against City, their next opportunity will come against a Chelsea side that have recently tightened up. No English top flight side have failed to score in their first six home games but Forest setting this unwanted record looks distinctly possible.

New Warzone mode lets you buy Nuke for $1,000,000 to end the game

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Warzone is making it easier than ever to get your hands on a Nuke, with a new Season 1 mode allowing players to purchase one for $1 million in-game.

Nukes are nothing new to Warzone, having featured in previous versions of the battle royale as a reward for completing the Champion’s Quest.

However, Season 1 introduces a much more straightforward way of obtaining one, allowing players to save up enough Cash and buy it directly from a Buy Station.

The feature is part of Rally Point, a brand-new mode arriving with Modern Warfare 4’s integration, which acts as a reintroduction to Warzone. Unlike traditional Battle Royale, Rally Point takes place on the new Zodiac map, supports up to 20 players, and doesn’t feature a shrinking gas circle.

It also offers unlimited respawns and a drop-in, drop-out system, meaning players can continue fighting and completing objectives without worrying about being permanently eliminated.

But those looking to bring the match to an end can work together to purchase a Nuke for $1 million from a Buy Station. Once bought, a countdown is triggered before a cutscene plays showing you and your squad evacuating as the map is destroyed.

You can buy a Tactical Nuke for $1,000,000 on the new Zodiac Warzone map and end the game for everyone ☢️ pic.twitter.com/UGN8iN3D7f

As mentioned in the Season 1 patch notes, the Killstreak is sold on a first-come, first-served basis, meaning only the quickest squad to raise enough money can secure it.

As always, players can earn Cash by completing contracts, eliminating enemies, and taking part in activities around the map.

Rally Point also features designated safe areas where players can change loadouts, receive Cash payouts, or team up with others. The Nuke provides a way to end the match, rather than continuing to fight indefinitely.

Chris Wilder must reintroduce Sheffield United award-winner vs Charlton Athletic after new injury update: Opinion

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Sheffield United were victorious in the week as they defeated Preston North End 3-2. It was Sydie Peck who stole the headlines with his first-half hat-trick, and he was partnered in midfield by Jairo Riedewald in the absence of SUFC’s reigning player of the month, Kalvin Phillips.

Riedewald has had a tough season to date, but he acted as the deeper-sitting man in the middle, keeping the tempo and guarding the backline while Peck was allowed to push on and break into goalscoring positions. It was a steady display from the Dutchman, who had not started a Championship game since September 5th.

However, ahead of the upcoming clash with Charlton Athletic, Chris Wilder has confirmed that Phillips has been passed fit and will travel with the squad down to London. If he is deemed ready to do so, he must be put straight back into the Blades’ XI.

After injury prematurely ended his first Sheffield United loan stay, Phillips has found an influential role over the past two months. The former Leeds United star is usually the deepest-sitting of the starting midfielders, guarding the backline and looking for long balls into the channels.

His 9.58 defensive contributions per 90 ranks him in the top 3% of Championship midfielders (FotMob), and he stands out in duels too. Phillips averages 7.84 duels won (top 9%) and 2.32 aerial duels won (top 5%) per match, and his passing range is shown by 3.63 accurate long balls (top 9%) too.

Phillips acts as the shield for the backline while his midfield partner is usually given freedom to venture forward and be more expressive. Peck has been operating in this role for the most part, and he was at his best in the midweek triumph over Preston North End.

Wilder has been using more of a 4-4-2 setup in recent weeks, and Phillips and Peck make for an excellently balanced partnership. It is one that could bring SUFC real success over the course of this season.

Phillips’ injury problems are well known, so it could be that Sheffield United lean on the side of caution and opt against throwing him straight back into the XI against Charlton Athletic.

If that is the case, Riedewald should keep his spot. He is similar to Phillips in his defensive play style, although he perhaps doesn’t boast the same technical ability or passing range.

Peck’s success at Deepdale showed Riedewald is a good facilitator though, and Wilder should not hesitate to call on him again.

Hamza Choudhury is an alternative option, but he has been needed at right-back with Femi Seriki expected to work his way back to full fitness after the international break.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Right-back sparked armed police response over incident

Expected to start Newcastle’s trip to Fulham on Saturday

Newcastle’s Amar Dedic is unlikely to face a club sanction after being spotted holding a bow and arrow in the Northumberland countryside and sparking a major police pursuit involving an armed response unit.

Dedic is expected to start Newcastle’s match at Fulham on Saturday following an incident that at one point saw six patrol cars pursue the Bosnia right-back back to his home on the upmarket Darras Hall estate, just outside Ponteland.

Northumbria Police were alerted on Wednesday afternoon when a member of the public spotted Dedic and a friend walking through the Northumberland countryside equipped with bows and arrows. This equipment was eventually seized from the boot of the 24-year-old’s car when he arrived back home at Darras Hall.

In a statement the force said they were satisfied the bows and arrows were “for recreational use and there was no threat to the public”, but inquiries were “on-going”. Pursuing animals using bow and arrows or crossbows is illegal in the UK but there are no suggestions that Dedic or his friend were on a hunting expedition.

“Shortly after 1.55pm on Wednesday we received a report of two men carrying a bow and arrow while walking along a track by the reservoir on the Military Road in the Harlow Hill area,” said Northumbria police. “Officers attended and subsequently stopped a vehicle in the Darras Hall area and seized bows and arrows.”

Singapore Airlines Unveils New Suites With Double Beds—Rollout Slips Again, And Many Planes Miss Out

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Singapore Airlines has unveiled new first class suites with double beds and a business class seat that finally fixes two of the most frustrating things about its long-haul product: having to flip the seat over to make a bed, and sleeping diagonally with your feet tucked into a corner. First class is coming to the ultra-long-range Airbus A350s that operate the world’s longest flights. Business class gets doors, a different sleeping position, and more foot space. We’re not going to see the first aircraft with the new cabins until the second half of 2027, and several fleets won’t ever get these seats at all. I flew to Singapore in November 2017 for the unveiling of their last new A380 business class and Suites. Nine years later, plenty of their aircraft still have an older product. And even the 380 itself didn’t finish installation until late 2023. Long haul A350 and Boeing 777 seats date to 2013. I flew to Seattle for delivery of the first 777 that had them. The Biggest Business Class Improvement Is Sleeping Singapore’s new business class was designed with BMW’s Designworks and built by Recaro. It’s a bespoke seat, with a forward-facing, staggered 1-2-1 layout with doors and direct aisle access throughout. The biggest improvement is being able to adjust the seat continuously from upright to flat, choose a comfortable lounging position, and sleep without angling my body across the seat. The new features include: Sliding doors and adjustable center dividers. Selected center pairs can become companion beds. This isn’t every middle pair, and it isn’t Qatar’s four-person Qsuite arrangement. A 79-inch bed and a larger footwell. The tray table stows at an angle under the screen, freeing space for your feet. Adjustable lumbar and leg support, plus more cushioning. Padded bed covers incorporate memory foam. More usable storage and charging. There are compartments beside the seat and screen, wireless phone charging, USB-C ports and universal power outlets. Updated entertainment and controls. Bluetooth headphone pairing, 4K touchscreens, spatial audio and saved seat preferences are part of the new experience. The A350 seat goes from 28 inches wide to 22 inches. On the wider Boeing 777-9, it’ll be 24 inches. So that’s a real tradeoff in seat width. Singapore’s old seat was exceptionally wide, but short and you had to angle your feet. A350 business class gets a 22-inch OLED screen, while the new 777-9 gets a smaller 20-inch screen without OLED. First Class Double Suite For The Longest Flights The new first class, designed by PriestmanGoode and manufactured by Collins Aerospace, will have four seats on the ultra-long-range A350 and six on the 777-9. The center pair can remain two independent suites or open into one shared space. Each passenger still has their own entrance, screen and table. Those seats can form a sofa for two or an 80-by-60-inch double bed. That’s the size of a standard U.S. queen mattress. Singapore calls it the largest double bed in commercial aviation. Individually, the beds measure 80 by 30 inches. The seats are 23 inches wide with the armrests raised. Curved sliding doors, warm colors, Poltrona Frau leather and batik details make this look much more like Singapore Airlines than the new business cabin. Companion dining is available at every first class seat, using the ottoman, which restores something missing from the current A380 Suites experience. The joined center suites can accommodate four people dining together. Other features include a personal wardrobe, an illuminated vanity drawer, carry-on storage beneath the ottoman, wireless charging and a 32-inch 4K screen. Bang & Olufsen wireless headphones are supplied, and you can pair your own headphones. A small information panel shows flight details and lavatory availability without requiring you to turn on the main screen. Seat and lighting preferences can be saved for future flights. This isn’t the A380’s separate armchair-and-bed concept. The chair becomes the bed. It’s a massive improvement over today’s 777 first class, while the A380 retains its current expansive Suites layout. There is only one center pair on each aircraft. Six first class seats on the 777-9 means four window singles and one potential double suite, not three double beds. Which Planes Get Which Seats The announced program covers 41 existing A350s and Singapore’s 31 ordered Boeing 777-9s. Only the 777-9 gets all four new seat designs. Aircraft   Configuration After The Changes   What It Gets 34 long-haul A350-900s   42 business, 24 premium economy, 192 economy   New business seats; existing premium economy and economy seats refreshed. No first class. 7 ultra-long-range A350-900s   4 first, 70 business, 58 premium economy   New first and business seats; existing premium economy seats refreshed. No economy cabin. 31 ordered Boeing 777-9s   6 first, 54 business, 36 premium economy, 204 economy   All four new cabins, on new aircraft. https://viewfromthewing.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/firstclasscabinwalkthrough.mp4 The A380, existing 777-300ER, medium-haul A350, 787-10 and 737-8 aren’t included in this seat program. Those aircraft keep their current cabin products under the announced plan. The A380 is separately getting Starlink. The rollout has already slipped twice: the first refurbished long-haul A350 was originally due in the second quarter of 2026, then the first quarter of 2027. Now it’s the second half of 2027. The airline still targets the end of 2030 to complete the A350 retrofits. Boeing’s delays explain why seats originally intended for the 777-9 are going onto A350s. But seat certification is also holding things up: the 777-9 seats have been certified, while the airplane hasn’t. The A350 seats still need certification. The ultra-long range A350 doesn’t have a firm separate debut date or announced launch route for its new first class. Premium Economy Improves On The 777-9. Economy Gets Denser With Another Seat Across. The new premium economy seat offers 40 inches of pitch, up from 38, and nine inches of recline instead of eight. It’s 19 inches wide, in a 2-4-2 cabin, with adjustable leg and footrests. It gets a 16-inch 4K screen, Bluetooth audio, USB-C and power outlets, plus a separate cocktail tray, a device holder and additional storage. Factorydesign styled both this cabin and economy, with Collins Aerospace manufacturing the seats. That’s more legroom and a better screen, but the seat doesn’t gain the substantial privacy wings or shell that some passengers were hoping for. 777-9 economy cabin goes to 3-4-3 and 31 to 32 inches of pitch. It’s a wider cabin, but Singapore’s current 777-300ERs are 3-3-3. There are redesigned cushions, adjustable headrests and a revised recline mechanism intended to improve knee and shin clearance, plus 13-inch 4K screens, Bluetooth and USB-C. Meanwhile, an A350 retrofit won’t bring these new premium economy or economy seats. Those cabins get refreshed upholstery and an updated entertainment interface, while keeping their existing seat hardware. There will even be two hardware versions within the 777-9 fleet. Premium economy and economy on the first 12 aircraft get conventional 4K screens. The later 19 get OLED screens but lose USB-A. Economy also loses universal power outlets on those 19 aircraft, retaining USB-C. Premium economy keeps universal power. On those later jets, coach will have OLED while first and business won’t. First Class Is Getting Praise. Business Class Is Getting Compared To Scoot. The early reaction is much warmer toward first class than business. In the FlyerTalk discussion folks like the larger bed, the return of companion dining and the ability to separate the middle pair into two proper suites. That’s a better arrangement for unrelated passengers than Lufthansa’s shared center suite. The business class thread is much rougher. Gray surfaces and bright yellow trim draw comparisons to Scoot, car interiors and a loss of Singapore’s warmth. Reddit reactions echo the complaints about generic styling and lost identity. Still, folks like the doors, storage and companion beds. Several Other Changes Arrive Before The Seats Singapore’s broader announcement bundles together upgrades with different schedules: Sleepwear and amenity kits. New kits begin rolling out this month on flights over six hours: Sisley in Suites and first, Byredo in business, INK & WATER in premium economy, and travel essentials in a batik pouch in economy. Business class gets pajamas for the first time, on all long-haul flights and selected overnight medium-haul flights over six hours. First class and Suites get new cotton sleepwear. Dining. The package includes more than 100 new or refreshed dishes, expanded drinks and Christofle serviceware in Suites, first and business. Lounge and ground-service improvements are also part of the broader investment. Entertainment and internet. The next KrisWorld system adds easier navigation, personalization, a wellness planner and more than 2,500 entertainment options, with hardware varying by cabin. Starlink begins rolling out from early 2027 on the A380 and long-haul and ultra-long-range A350s. The announced Starlink list doesn’t include the 777-9. The Biggest Commercial Change Is At The Back Of The Longest Flights The ultra-long-range A350 goes from 67 business and 94 premium economy seats to four first, 70 business and 58 premium economy. That’s 36 fewer premium economy seats or a 38% cut. Total passenger capacity falls from 161 to 132, or 18%. Singapore is replacing a substantial portion of its lowest-priced cabin on these aircraft with space it expects to sell for more. First class provides a new way to monetize the nonstop flights. Net net I expect this to mean tougher award space overall (certainly in premium economy). Topics on this pageAirbus A350-900Aircraft cabinBoeing 777XBusiness classAirbusAirbus A350Airbus A380Bang & OlufsenBluetoothBoeingBoeing 777Collins AerospaceFlyerTalkLufthansaQatarRedditScootSeattleSingaporeSingapore AirlinesStarlinkSuite+18 more More From View from the Wing

First class is coming to the ultra-long-range Airbus A350s that operate the world’s longest flights. Business class gets doors, a different sleeping position, and more foot space. We’re not going to see the first aircraft with the new cabins until the second half of 2027, and several fleets won’t ever get these seats at all.

I flew to Singapore in November 2017 for the unveiling of their last new A380 business class and Suites. Nine years later, plenty of their aircraft still have an older product. And even the 380 itself didn’t finish installation until late 2023. Long haul A350 and Boeing 777 seats date to 2013. I flew to Seattle for delivery of the first 777 that had them.

Singapore’s new business class was designed with BMW’s Designworks and built by Recaro. It’s a bespoke seat, with a forward-facing, staggered 1-2-1 layout with doors and direct aisle access throughout. The biggest improvement is being able to adjust the seat continuously from upright to flat, choose a comfortable lounging position, and sleep without angling my body across the seat. The new features include: Sliding doors and adjustable center dividers. Selected center pairs can become companion beds. This isn’t every middle pair, and it isn’t Qatar’s four-person Qsuite arrangement. A 79-inch bed and a larger footwell. The tray table stows at an angle under the screen, freeing space for your feet. Adjustable lumbar and leg support, plus more cushioning. Padded bed covers incorporate memory foam. More usable storage and charging. There are compartments beside the seat and screen, wireless phone charging, USB-C ports and universal power outlets. Updated entertainment and controls. Bluetooth headphone pairing, 4K touchscreens, spatial audio and saved seat preferences are part of the new experience. The A350 seat goes from 28 inches wide to 22 inches. On the wider Boeing 777-9, it’ll be 24 inches. So that’s a real tradeoff in seat width. Singapore’s old seat was exceptionally wide, but short and you had to angle your feet. A350 business class gets a 22-inch OLED screen, while the new 777-9 gets a smaller 20-inch screen without OLED. First Class Double Suite For The Longest Flights The new first class, designed by PriestmanGoode and manufactured by Collins Aerospace, will have four seats on the ultra-long-range A350 and six on the 777-9. The center pair can remain two independent suites or open into one shared space. Each passenger still has their own entrance, screen and table. Those seats can form a sofa for two or an 80-by-60-inch double bed. That’s the size of a standard U.S. queen mattress. Singapore calls it the largest double bed in commercial aviation. Individually, the beds measure 80 by 30 inches. The seats are 23 inches wide with the armrests raised. Curved sliding doors, warm colors, Poltrona Frau leather and batik details make this look much more like Singapore Airlines than the new business cabin. Companion dining is available at every first class seat, using the ottoman, which restores something missing from the current A380 Suites experience. The joined center suites can accommodate four people dining together. Other features include a personal wardrobe, an illuminated vanity drawer, carry-on storage beneath the ottoman, wireless charging and a 32-inch 4K screen. Bang & Olufsen wireless headphones are supplied, and you can pair your own headphones. A small information panel shows flight details and lavatory availability without requiring you to turn on the main screen. Seat and lighting preferences can be saved for future flights. This isn’t the A380’s separate armchair-and-bed concept. The chair becomes the bed. It’s a massive improvement over today’s 777 first class, while the A380 retains its current expansive Suites layout. There is only one center pair on each aircraft. Six first class seats on the 777-9 means four window singles and one potential double suite, not three double beds. Which Planes Get Which Seats The announced program covers 41 existing A350s and Singapore’s 31 ordered Boeing 777-9s. Only the 777-9 gets all four new seat designs. Aircraft   Configuration After The Changes   What It Gets 34 long-haul A350-900s   42 business, 24 premium economy, 192 economy   New business seats; existing premium economy and economy seats refreshed. No first class. 7 ultra-long-range A350-900s   4 first, 70 business, 58 premium economy   New first and business seats; existing premium economy seats refreshed. No economy cabin. 31 ordered Boeing 777-9s   6 first, 54 business, 36 premium economy, 204 economy   All four new cabins, on new aircraft. https://viewfromthewing.com/wp-content/uploads/2026/11/firstclasscabinwalkthrough.mp4 The A380, existing 777-300ER, medium-haul A350, 787-10 and 737-8 aren’t included in this seat program. Those aircraft keep their current cabin products under the announced plan. The A380 is separately getting Starlink. The rollout has already slipped twice: the first refurbished long-haul A350 was originally due in the second quarter of 2026, then the first quarter of 2027. Now it’s the second half of 2027. The airline still targets the end of 2030 to complete the A350 retrofits. Boeing’s delays explain why seats originally intended for the 777-9 are going onto A350s. But seat certification is also holding things up: the 777-9 seats have been certified, while the airplane hasn’t. The A350 seats still need certification. The ultra-long range A350 doesn’t have a firm separate debut date or announced launch route for its new first class. Premium Economy Improves On The 777-9. Economy Gets Denser With Another Seat Across. The new premium economy seat offers 40 inches of pitch, up from 38, and nine inches of recline instead of eight. It’s 19 inches wide, in a 2-4-2 cabin, with adjustable leg and footrests. It gets a 16-inch 4K screen, Bluetooth audio, USB-C and power outlets, plus a separate cocktail tray, a device holder and additional storage. Factorydesign styled both this cabin and economy, with Collins Aerospace manufacturing the seats. That’s more legroom and a better screen, but the seat doesn’t gain the substantial privacy wings or shell that some passengers were hoping for. 777-9 economy cabin goes to 3-4-3 and 31 to 32 inches of pitch. It’s a wider cabin, but Singapore’s current 777-300ERs are 3-3-3. There are redesigned cushions, adjustable headrests and a revised recline mechanism intended to improve knee and shin clearance, plus 13-inch 4K screens, Bluetooth and USB-C. Meanwhile, an A350 retrofit won’t bring these new premium economy or economy seats. Those cabins get refreshed upholstery and an updated entertainment interface, while keeping their existing seat hardware. There will even be two hardware versions within the 777-9 fleet. Premium economy and economy on the first 12 aircraft get conventional 4K screens. The later 19 get OLED screens but lose USB-A. Economy also loses universal power outlets on those 19 aircraft, retaining USB-C. Premium economy keeps universal power. On those later jets, coach will have OLED while first and business won’t. First Class Is Getting Praise. Business Class Is Getting Compared To Scoot. The early reaction is much warmer toward first class than business. In the FlyerTalk discussion folks like the larger bed, the return of companion dining and the ability to separate the middle pair into two proper suites. That’s a better arrangement for unrelated passengers than Lufthansa’s shared center suite. The business class thread is much rougher. Gray surfaces and bright yellow trim draw comparisons to Scoot, car interiors and a loss of Singapore’s warmth. Reddit reactions echo the complaints about generic styling and lost identity. Still, folks like the doors, storage and companion beds. Several Other Changes Arrive Before The Seats Singapore’s broader announcement bundles together upgrades with different schedules: Sleepwear and amenity kits. New kits begin rolling out this month on flights over six hours: Sisley in Suites and first, Byredo in business, INK & WATER in premium economy, and travel essentials in a batik pouch in economy. Business class gets pajamas for the first time, on all long-haul flights and selected overnight medium-haul flights over six hours. First class and Suites get new cotton sleepwear. Dining. The package includes more than 100 new or refreshed dishes, expanded drinks and Christofle serviceware in Suites, first and business. Lounge and ground-service improvements are also part of the broader investment. Entertainment and internet. The next KrisWorld system adds easier navigation, personalization, a wellness planner and more than 2,500 entertainment options, with hardware varying by cabin. Starlink begins rolling out from early 2027 on the A380 and long-haul and ultra-long-range A350s. The announced Starlink list doesn’t include the 777-9. The Biggest Commercial Change Is At The Back Of The Longest Flights The ultra-long-range A350 goes from 67 business and 94 premium economy seats to four first, 70 business and 58 premium economy. That’s 36 fewer premium economy seats or a 38% cut. Total passenger capacity falls from 161 to 132, or 18%. Singapore is replacing a substantial portion of its lowest-priced cabin on these aircraft with space it expects to sell for more. First class provides a new way to monetize the nonstop flights. Net net I expect this to mean tougher award space overall (certainly in premium economy). The A380, existing 777-300ER, medium-haul A350, 787-10 and 737-8 aren’t included in this seat program. Those aircraft keep their current cabin products under the announced plan. The A380 is separately getting Starlink.

The rollout has already slipped twice: the first refurbished long-haul A350 was originally due in the second quarter of 2026, then the first quarter of 2027. Now it’s the second half of 2027. The airline still targets the end of 2030 to complete the A350 retrofits.

Boeing’s delays explain why seats originally intended for the 777-9 are going onto A350s. But seat certification is also holding things up: the 777-9 seats have been certified, while the airplane hasn’t. The A350 seats still need certification. The ultra-long range A350 doesn’t have a firm separate debut date or announced launch route for its new first class.

The new premium economy seat offers 40 inches of pitch, up from 38, and nine inches of recline instead of eight. It’s 19 inches wide, in a 2-4-2 cabin, with adjustable leg and footrests. It gets a 16-inch 4K screen, Bluetooth audio, USB-C and power outlets, plus a separate cocktail tray, a device holder and additional storage. Factorydesign styled both this cabin and economy, with Collins Aerospace manufacturing the seats. That’s more legroom and a better screen, but the seat doesn’t gain the substantial privacy wings or shell that some passengers were hoping for. 777-9 economy cabin goes to 3-4-3 and 31 to 32 inches of pitch. It’s a wider cabin, but Singapore’s current 777-300ERs are 3-3-3. There are redesigned cushions, adjustable headrests and a revised recline mechanism intended to improve knee and shin clearance, plus 13-inch 4K screens, Bluetooth and USB-C. Meanwhile, an A350 retrofit won’t bring these new premium economy or economy seats. Those cabins get refreshed upholstery and an updated entertainment interface, while keeping their existing seat hardware. There will even be two hardware versions within the 777-9 fleet. Premium economy and economy on the first 12 aircraft get conventional 4K screens. The later 19 get OLED screens but lose USB-A. Economy also loses universal power outlets on those 19 aircraft, retaining USB-C. Premium economy keeps universal power. On those later jets, coach will have OLED while first and business won’t. First Class Is Getting Praise. Business Class Is Getting Compared To Scoot. The early reaction is much warmer toward first class than business. In the FlyerTalk discussion folks like the larger bed, the return of companion dining and the ability to separate the middle pair into two proper suites. That’s a better arrangement for unrelated passengers than Lufthansa’s shared center suite. The business class thread is much rougher. Gray surfaces and bright yellow trim draw comparisons to Scoot, car interiors and a loss of Singapore’s warmth. Reddit reactions echo the complaints about generic styling and lost identity. Still, folks like the doors, storage and companion beds. Tony Pulis’ Napoleon adoration was not what we expected to read this morning

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tony Pulis has provided a quite fascinating insight into his mind as he went into great detail about why Napoleon Bonaparte is one of his idols.

Since retiring from managing, Pulis has been doing a lot of media work, including his own podcast, but he also writes a regular column for the BBC.

The subject of this week’s entry wasn’t really anything to do with football and more to do with French emperor Napoleon Bonaparte.

Inside the excellently titled column ‘Why managers can learn a lot from leaders like Napoleon’, Pulis reveals how he first became obsessed with the French dictator after leaving Gillingham in 1990.

“Whatever I was doing, I always felt like I was learning, and not just by watching football around the world. I became an avid reader of sports books and also finding out about great leaders of men through history.

“So, as well as visiting Bayer Leverkusen, Athletic Bilbao and RB Leipzig – who back then had only just started their rise from the bottom tier of German football – I would analyse and investigate historic battles or events.”

I mean, that is just a fantastic paragraph isn’t it? That is full middle-aged living his best life. We presume he mixed this with playing golf and running a marathon.

“Napoleon Bonaparte really kept me busy. His attention to detail and his command of his generals was only superseded by his troops’ loyalty to him – they would literally die for him!”

Not to correct you Tony, but troops dying for their leaders is quite the point of the whole thing isn’t it? Perhaps a better example to use was when Napoleon returned from his first exile and when met with a battalion, they instead joined him rather than stopping him. Much like your return to Stoke.

And to be fair, exile on a remote island must be what managers out of work feel like, except Napoleon did not have the life raft of an appearance on Soccer Saturday.

“His 250th birthday was celebrated in Corsica in the summer of 2019, and a French newspaper invited me to go along and give a British perspective on how we see Napoleon as a leader and a person.”

Right, so there is a lot to unpack here and Pulis is quite vague on the details so we felt compelled to find out more.

It turns out that a reporter for Le Monde, a French newspaper, happened to discover that Pulis was a fan of historical world leaders and in particular Napoleon so invited him over.

Pulis, who was still managing Middlesbrough at the time we should add, travelled over to Napoleon’s birthday palace where he met a man who was 6ft 6’ and a direct descendant of Napoleon.

We are sure we can all agree that it is one of the great travesties of life that Pulis did not offer him a contract to play at centre-back and rob us of the chance to have Bonaparte marshalling the defence. He would probably be too focused on attack, though.

“Hated by many countries, but still adored by his followers, Napoleon also produced the Napoleonic Code, which is still the foundation of French civil law today.”

READ: Tony Pulis saying “I Know Him” about everyone is Peak Football Dad & weirdly comforting

Umm yes that’s right Tony but he was also responsible for up to 6.5 million deaths so swings and roundabouts. So annoying was he in fact that the British felt compelled to expel him to a remote island. Twice.

Much like Pulis’, Napoleon’s fall was expanding too quickly across Europe. The Battle of Waterloo is pretty much the same as Stoke being knocked out of the Europa League by Valencia in the round of 32. They finished 14th that season.

It’s not just Napoleon Pulis has a love-in for and when he’s not on the sidelines, it would appear he is travelling to remote parts of the world where battles once took place.

“One summer, I travelled out to Crimea to witness the scene of that tragic charge by the bravest of brave men,” he said of the Charge of the Light Brigade, which was narrowly ahead of the Britannia on a Tuesday night in the list of difficult places to get a result.

Pulis ends with some advice to his successors.

“You should never be afraid of dropping down a level the way I did, too. It is better to get a job, any job, than be out of football full-stop. That hasn’t changed since my day, either.”

Tell that to Sean Dyche who is too busy during Durex ads.
