This simple fix will ‘unleash’ West Ham talent after costly Nuno cock-up | Report

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

West Ham United’s treatment of loanees hasn’t always been well received by their parent clubs. It’d be doubtful that Brighton would ever want to entertain sending another prospect out on loan to West Ham, after the experiences endured by Igor Julio and Evan Ferguson whose careers nosedived after they’d donned the claret and blue shirt for their photo shoots: Neither were seen again for the season.

However. this time around Manchester City must be delighted that the Irons are getting excellent value for money out of loanee Divine Mukasa, even if they weren’t utilising him to his best in Nuno’s favoured, deeper midfield role.

Manchester City fan site esteemedkompany.com, even from afar, identified that the midfielder’s deployment by Nuno as a deep lying defensive option was not playing to his strengths:

“There is an argument to be made that Nuno Espirito Santo played Mukasa out of position….

“Parker utilized Mukasa as an attacking midfielder in the match…

Mukasa scored West Ham’s first goal in a remarkable comeback

Mukasa then produced a sublime chip to beat Cardiff City keeper Yousef Salech, setting the stage for West Ham’s comeback. That goal illustrated what Mukasa can do playing in arguably his best position.….

Scott Parker may decide to unleash Mukasa as an attacking midfielder”

You’d hope that is one of the easier decisions for Scott Parker and his team to take: Mukasa already has more goals than our so-called strikers Taty and Pablo – combined. Which doesn’t say much about the other two, but surely means the young loanee’s best deployment is exactly as he finished the game against Cardiff City.

Parker has plenty of options as deeper-lying midfielders just sitting ignored by Nuno for months – blow away the cobwebs on Alvarez, Magassa (who did look rusty) or Orford and any one of the three can, with a little bit of integration, do that job and allow the hugely talented Manchester City loanee to do more of just what he’s here for.

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

This Swanky Service Lets Commercial Fliers Skip the Airport Entirely—And It's Now Available in Dallas and Miami

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

As my plane touched down in Dallas, I could already make out my chauffeur waiting on the apron, the jet-black SUV glimmering in the Texas sun. It wasn’t long before my luggage was whisked away and we were driving across the airfield, past rows of planes waiting at their gates—so close that I could read their tail numbers. Our destination? A new private terminal operated by the company PS, which offers a curious promise to commercial fliers: Travel with us, and you will never step foot in the airport.

The shared “Salon” area at the PS Dallas Terminal, included in the entry-level pricing. Access to private suites cost an additional fee based on membership status.

Ten minutes later, my driver dropped me off at PS’s sleek new 12,200-square-foot private terminal at Dallas Fort Worth International Airport (DFW), where I was greeted by caviar and Champagne and escorted to my own private suite. Between the goodie-filled drawers, marble sinks, and the rainfall shower, I could have spent my whole time in the bathroom, where I had my pick of Le Labo products, plush towels, and comfy robes. It wasn’t like any airport lounge I had ever been to, rivaling even first class. And yet, it's still a fraction of the cost of flying private.

Champagne and caviar at the PS private suite.

PS first launched at Los Angeles International Airport (LAX) in 2017, then called “The Private Suite.” As the name implies, all PS terminals have their own private, line-free TSA security screenings and dedicated Customs and Immigration checkpoints separate from the crowds. While this has earned the brand a reputation of being a “celebrity terminal," PS CEO Amina Belouizdad Porter says public figures actually make up "a very, very small portion" of the customer base. The real clientele are premium business travelers, families, and travelers going for celebratory splurges, she says.

"If you fly from LA to New York and back in first class, these days, that's around $4,000. If you do that private, that's probably $100,000,” Porter explains. “The gap is so big, and there are many people that want to bridge that."

For the last decade, PS has continued to expand. After renaming itself to PS in 2020, it opened a terminal at Hartsfield-Jackson Atlanta International Airport (ATL) in 2023. The PS terminal at DFW opened on June 1 and was quickly followed by another PS location at Miami International Airport (MIA) later that month. This October, I flew between the two new terminals to get a better idea of how the exclusive service works behind the scenes—and who it’s actually for.

Each private suite has a bathroom complete with Le Labo products, plush towels, and robes.

In Porter’s view, departing is the most stressful part of air travel. "That's when you're asking, 'Where am I going? What time is my plane? Where's my gate?'" she says. Arrivals or transfers can be just as confusing for many travelers: going through immigration and customs, dealing with heavy bags, finding the right taxi stand or connecting to the right terminal for the next flight. The point of PS is to make both legs of the airport journey as seamless as possible.

After I was welcomed at the PS facility at Dallas-Fort Worth and spent a night in the city, I experienced the departure product en route to Miami. I once again rolled up in a chauffeured car to the PS terminal, where caviar was (naturally) waiting for me. The Dallas terminal occupies a gated facility adjacent to Corporate Aviation at DFW and was designed by interior designer Jean Liu to feel residential, like a luxury hotel.

The 40-foot bar in “The Salon” at the Dallas PS terminal.

I spent much of my pre-flight time in the public lounge area dubbed “the Salon,” which had floor-to-ceiling windows looking out over the tarmac. This area hosts a stunning 40-foot bar, taking inspiration from an old-fashioned Southern saloon. Once I settled in, I ordered breakfast (in Dallas, PS executive chef Matt Roman's blue cornbread is a must), and then worked and read a magazine. My boarding pass came with a sheet telling me when to be ready to board, though the PS staff guides make sure you are always where you need to be regardless.

Behind the scenes, the Control Room tracks all flights, delays, arrivals, and departures. From there, PS coordinates with the airline, TSA, and Customs and Border Protection, and stays in direct contact with the flight crews, which is how they’ll know your flight is delayed before the passengers at the gate do.

When it was time for me to board, I went through security right inside the PS terminal. It's a real TSA checkpoint, because PS is the only company in the United States with a private partnership with both TSA and CBP. Guests go through one at a time, and if you check a bag, PS checks it in with the airline for you. Once again, a car drove me to my plane, and I walked up a set of steps outside the jet bridge, through a door, and straight to my seat.

The open-air courtyard at the Miami PS terminal, located in the historic Pan Am headquarters.

By the time I got to Miami, I knew the PS drill. A sign with my name on it waited at the jet bridge, and from the plane it was a 15-minute drive to the original Pan Am headquarters.

At 34,000 square feet, the PS terminal in Miami is nearly three times the size of the one in Dallas—and the design could not be more different. That’s because the Miami terminal is a loving restoration: the building once housed both Pan Am's former headquarters and its stewardess college, where flight attendants trained in the 1960s. In March 2026, the buildings were added to the National Register of Historic Places as part of the “Pan American Airways Historic District," making the terminal a stunning architectural gem.

Pan Am’s Miami headquarters first opened in 1964, sat empty for 15 years, and was slated for demolition before PS restored it. The restoration shows off the New Formalist style (the bold, geometric designs of Miami's postwar resort boom), with an exterior wrapped in a patterned concrete screen and a repeating trapezoid design that reappears in the planters and door handles. The original insignias and gold paneling are still there, and going up the stairs, the Pan Am logo is engraved over and over into the stone wall.

The Miami PS terminal is filled with playful vintage touches, including palm tree wallpaper and capiz shell chandeliers.

Within the terminal, each PS suite has a different vibe (mine was pink), complete with nods to the beach and vintage touches, including palm tree wallpaper and capiz shell chandeliers. The courtyard is open-air, with a decorative pool tiled by Miami artist Nina Surel in Art Deco pastels that's lined with pink-and-white striped loungers and fringed umbrellas. This time, my Private Suite opened onto a balcony overlooking the courtyard. Inside was the same selection of amenities as in Dallas, plus a fresh food menu that was fitting for glitzy Miami: sashimi with cilantro dust, the classic caviar and Champagne, and—for good measure—a cubano. I enjoyed a chair massage, one of the spa's 30-minute treatments (including reflexology, manicures, hair and beard trims, and full-body massages). As much as I would have liked to linger, when it was time to go I took a sandwich to-go for my next flight, and left in PS style: Another car. Another plane.

Of course, none of this comes cheap. PS operates on a tiered membership system: The entry-level Salon Membership costs $1,250 annually, puts access to the Salon at $995 per person, and allows a member to book a Private Suite (accommodating up to four travelers) for $4,950. The top-tier All Access Membership is priced at $4,850 per year, and lowers access rates to the Salon to $895 per person, and Private Suite rates to $3,650. Without a membership, entry to the Salon costs $1,295 per person, and a Private Suite booking costs $4,950. Notably, because the Salon has an age requirement (21 and over, there’s alcohol), parties traveling with those underage are required to book a Private Suite. Pets are welcome in all PS private terminals, and all PS reservations must be made at least 24 hours ahead of travelers’ departure times.

Access to the private suites starts at $4,950 for members, with room for four travelers.

How should you decide if it's a worthwhile purchase for your trip? Porter is honest about which travelers should skip the whole thing: “If you're on your own, with no checked bags, going on an hour flight for the weekend, then the airport is just fine,” she says. "It makes the most sense for long flights and complicated routings; and parties with a lot of checked bags, kids, and older parents who could use the assistance.”

Because the concept blends private and commercial aviation in a way that hasn't been done before, Porter believes it's hard for travelers to fully understand the value of PS—until they've experienced it themselves. "It's just complete peace of mind," she tells me in Dallas. “That's what we're selling.”

As the most discerning, up-to-the-minute voice in all things travel, Condé Nast Traveler is the global citizen’s bible and muse, offering both inspiration and vital intel. We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.

Tate Britain’s ’90s Show Is All Scene, No Substance

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

There is a source of tension raging across the art world lately: the conflict between accessibility and anti-intellectualism, as museums face the challenge of making art feel less elitist, less cold, and less boring without dumbing down to the point of meaninglessness. Tate Britain’s latest exhibition, “The 90s: Art and Fashion,” falls hard on the side of meaningless anti-intellectualism.

The exhibition is curated by Edward Enninful, who was most recently editor-in-chief of British Vogue but, more importantly for this exhibition, became the fashion director of the magazine i-D at the age of 18 in 1990 and led it through the turbulent, dynamic decade that is the subject of his show. Tate has never worked with a celebrity curator in this way before, and Enninful’s presence is strangely felt throughout the exhibition. A wedding gift he received from Steve McQueen—a photograph of McQueen’s hands—hangs on a wall. Many, many i-D spreads are in cases throughout the galleries, some including photographs of Enninful. There is a striking focus on Black masculinity, which is the prism through which Enninful experienced the ’90s. Yet his subjectivity is not clearly articulated, leaving one to wonder: Is it his voice we are sensing through the wall texts and object labels, or is it a more neutral “museum” voice? Is Enninful the subject of the exhibition, via his presence in the lives of the artists and models represented here, or is he the curator? Related Articles 50 Artworks by Artists Who Lived and Worked in India Gifted to the Tate Tate Scoops Up Works by Michaela Yearwood-Dan, Luis Fernando Zapata at Frieze London

Being a museum curator is a job that does, actually, require expertise. (I speak with the bias of being one myself). It is a powerful, often invisible role that shapes perceptions of truth and cultural meaning. The trend toward treating it as a celebrity hobby or a passion project is symptomatic of the current wider social phobia of experts and intellectuals. Throughout this exhibition, Enninful contradicts himself: The decade of the ’90s in Britain was about “critiquing commercialism,” but it was also about groundbreaking ad campaigns. It was about “challenging beauty standards,” but also “democratizing beauty.” No point of distinction between fine art and visual culture is identified in Enninful’s show, so fashion, commercial and personal photography, and fine art share space. That could be interesting—it could be a critical choice—but instead it feels like an oversight.

The art and visual culture in this show generally does something. It either embraces or rejects commercialism. It tells a new, sometimes important, story of race, class, or gender in Britain. It highlights what one wall text ambiguously describes as “importance of intimacy in turbulent times.” But is it good? Not especially. Enninful seems to have gone out of his way to choose bad works. Helen Chadwick, a largely good artist, is represented by two works that rank among her weakest. A cast of a hot water bottle that represents a torso by Rachel Whiteread, a really good artist, somehow manages to be the best thing in the show (along with Chris Ofili’s 1998 painting No Woman, No Cry). Small, quotidian, and tactile, it evokes absence and presence at once, the bitter fleetingness of modern life.

But most of the stuff in these galleries? The endless snapshots of parties, wallpaper covered in butts, the many photographs of Kate Moss, videos of fashion shows, enlarged ad campaign stills—very little of it is good. It conveys a certain nostalgia for a heady era, and it makes a strong case for Enninful’s relevance to it. He knew everyone who is anyone, it seems. But that doesn’t make what he chose to show good art, or even art at all. It’s odd, because the ’90s were famously good years for British art: the time of the YBAs and a shifting and condensing sense of British identity, of optimism and progress. The decade fueled the founding of Tate Modern and Frieze London in the early 2000s, and has led to London’s current position as a nexus of international artists and remarkable historic and contemporary art spaces. You would not know that from this exhibition.

Art museums have to fight for relevance in our new attention economy, just like everything else. We are constantly asked to defend them, to make a case for their purpose. With this exhibition, Tate situates the museum alongside the celebrity economy of influence. Supposedly celebrating the last “analog” decade, it instead feels like an act of surrender to the perceived glibness of the digital age. Old-fashioned as it may seem, I don’t think we should equate art with “content.” There is always an appetite for great art, and there is also always an appetite to consider whether, and why, art is great or not. To join the tide of anti-intellectualism that refuses to take this seriously or rise to the challenge of doing so, as Tate has done with this exhibition, is a grave shame.

‘It’s H for Housing’: How Texas Democrats Plan To Build on GOP Homebuilding Momentum

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Texas enacted some of its most consequential housing supply reforms in years under Republican leadership, but Tuesday's historic Democratic sweep in the Lone Star State has suddenly opened the door to changes advocates thought were politically impossible.

“The boundaries shifted entirely for the possibilities for low-income households and Texans seeking affordability,” Texas Housers, a nonprofit housing advocacy organization, tells Realtor.com®. "What was realistic 24 hours ago is dramatically different from what is realistic today.”

Among those possibilities are greater state investment in affordable housing, stronger renter protections, and more funding to rebuild homes damaged by natural disasters—priorities the organization hopes the incoming administration will advance.

But the state's homebuilding industry is looking to build on an agenda it has spent years advancing with officials from both parties.

"It's neither D nor R. It's H for housing," Scott Norman, CEO of the Texas Association of Builders, tells Realtor.com.

After an election shaped by pocketbook concerns, Texas' new guard will doubtless be judged on whether it can solve for Norman's 'H'—turning the momentum of the nation's most active homebuilding market into homes more Texans can afford.

Texas' Republican-led Legislature had already begun dismantling barriers to housing construction before Tuesday's election.

Senate Bill 840, for example, allows developers to convert certain commercial properties into apartments without extensive rezoning.

Since taking effect in September 2025, the law has enabled plans for at least 8,400 apartments, according to a July analysis by the Texas Tribune citing a coalition of housing reform advocates. That's equivalent to roughly one-fifth of the multifamily units permitted statewide during the period.

That’s an especially notable share given that the Lone Star State leads the nation in homebuilding, issuing more permits than any other state and accounting for 14.6% of building permits issued nationwide, according to the latest Affordability and Homebuilding Report Cards from Realtor.com.

But to Norman's point, it was a bipartisan push—one that Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa helped advance during her time in the state Legislature. As a member of the House Land & Resource Management Committee, she voted in favor of moving SB 840 forward.

It's something Norman hasn’t forgotten, saying she was "supportive of stuff we were pushing" during the previous legislative session. "I'm sure and I know she'll take that experience to the governor's mansion.”

Tray Bates, vice president of governmental affairs at Texas Realtors, also sees room for cooperation.

"The core problem is supply," Bates tells Realtor.com. "Texas is adding people faster than it's adding homes people can afford."

Both organizations want to continue easing restrictions on residential development, while Norman also sees an opportunity to address the shortage of skilled construction workers through expanded workforce training.

"The more folks we can get in the business, that'll drive down those labor costs," he says.

But Texas' homebuilding boom has done far less to close the gap for its lowest-income renters.

The state faces a shortage of 709,000 affordable and available rentals for extremely low-income households, according to the National Low Income Housing Coalition.

Texas Housers argues that insufficient state investment has contributed to that shortfall.

"Texas has relied almost entirely on federal dollars and tax credits to create and preserve affordable housing in our communities," the organization says. "Current resources are not enough to address the need for safe, decent, and affordable housing."

Indeed, just $10.1 million in state funding was allocated to the Housing Trust Fund for fiscal years 2026 and 2027. By contrast, the state awarded more than $114 million in federal housing tax credits to help finance affordable rental units in 2026 alone.

The programs serve different purposes, to be sure, but the figures illustrate how heavily Texas relies on federal incentives to finance affordable rental development.

Land Commissioner-elect Ben Flores sees another opportunity in the approximately 13 million acres managed by the General Land Office.

"We [sic] hold parcels near growing cities that are sitting idle," Flores tells Realtor.com. "My team will inventory those parcels and identify which ones could support housing for teachers, nurses, first responders, and working families through long-term leases that keep the land in public hands and still earn revenue for the Permanent School Fund.

"Housing and school funding don't have to compete," he adds. "Done right, they support each other."

Flores intends to prioritize the Houston region and Rio Grande Valley, where he says rising housing costs have compounded the challenges facing families recovering from floods.

He's also promising to address delays in distributing disaster recovery aid—an issue Texas Housers says has left some Hurricane Harvey survivors waiting years to rebuild.

"When the state relies solely on federal dollars run through slow, unaccountable systems, the families with the least end up waiting the longest," the organization says.

Flores says he'll tackle this head-on in his first 90 days by conducting an agency audit, with public progress reports every 30 days and measurable targets for distributing assistance.

"We will review every open recovery program, find out where funds are stuck and why, and work to get those dollars back to hurricane survivors," Flores says. "Allocation decisions will be made based on need and damage, not politics, and we'll publish where the money is going so Texans can see it for themselves."

Renters could also see a different approach under the incoming administration.

"Basic tenant protections are also urgently needed," Texas Housers says, calling for eviction record sealing and greater transparency around rental fees among its legislative priorities.

Lt. Gov.-elect Vikki Goodwin has proposed property tax incentives for landlords who maintain affordable rents, while Attorney General-elect Nathan Johnson has promised a 30-day review of consumer protection and competition laws, specifically identifying algorithmic rent-setting as an enforcement concern.

Then there are the forces outside what any single public office can control—forces that Norman argues will be the greater determinants of Texas' housing future.

"Two things that drive housing growth are population growth and job growth," he says.

And there’s been no shortage of that in Texas. The state has added more than 2.6 million residents since 2020, according to U.S. Census Bureau figures.

When asked about fears that growth could stall or reverse, Norman responds with enthusiastic optimism that the state's economic appeal will continue attracting households and supporting construction.

But some real estate professionals outside the state are watching for signs that the political shift could change where affluent residents choose to live.

Miltiadis Kastanis, a Miami Beach luxury real estate agent with Compass, says inquiries from Texas buyers were rising before the election.

"We have noticed an increase in inquiries from the Texan market," he says.

Kastanis believes the latest political shifts could accelerate that interest, particularly among wealthy Texans concerned about future changes to the state's tax environment.

"You will see Texans start pre-planning a move to the Florida market," he says, but he cautions against expecting a mass exodus or COVID-19 pandemic-era bidding wars.

Bates is similarly skeptical that one election will significantly alter migration patterns.

"It's far too early to predict migration based on one election," he says. "Domestic migration to Texas was already slowing before Tuesday, and housing costs are a major factor."

And at a crossroads for Texas housing policy, that could become a North Star for housing advocates, industry leaders, and the incoming administration alike.

"Maybe the policy solutions may look a little different based on who's in charge," Norman says, but the commitment to a more affordable Texas remains the same.

"There's a lot we can get done with the new folks coming in," he says.

Allaire Conte is a senior advice writer covering real estate and personal finance trends. She previously served as deputy editor of home services at CNN Underscored Money and was a lead writer at Orchard, where she simplified complex real estate topics for everyday readers. She holds an MFA in Nonfiction Writing from Columbia University and a BFA in Writing, Literature, and Publishing from Emerson College. When she’s not writing about homeownership hurdles and housing market shifts, she’s biking around Brooklyn or baking cakes for her friends.

Bitcoin and Ethereum ETFs lose $530 million in one day as $3 trillion Wall Street wipeout rattles crypto

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

BlackRock and Fidelity lead withdrawals as the Nasdaq suffers its steepest decline since the pandemic.

US spot Bitcoin and Ethereum ETFs suffered $530 million in combined withdrawals Thursday as a Wall Street rout erased their post-midterm gains.

Data from Farside Investors showed that Bitcoin ETFs lost $430.23 million on Nov. 5, while their Ethereum counterparts recorded another $99.98 million in outflows.

Daily US spot ETF net flows, not assets under management. Two-decimal values reconcile to the user-supplied fund list; Farside independently lists rounded one-decimal totals of -$430.2M for Bitcoin and -$99.9M for Ethereum. Additional precision is unverified.

Source: Farside Bitcoin ETF flows, Nov. 5, 2026 · Farside Ethereum ETF flows, Nov. 5, 2026

The latest ETF withdrawals extended a broader retreat that had begun before Thursday's market turbulence.

BlackRock's iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded $105 million in outflows, marking its fifth consecutive trading session of withdrawals.

Over that period, investors pulled approximately $1.04 billion from the fund, including $405.3 million on Nov. 2 alone.

Across all US spot Bitcoin ETFs, cumulative net withdrawals reached approximately $1.51 billion over the five sessions since Oct. 30, despite Wednesday's brief return to positive flows.

Fidelity's FBTC suffered the biggest withdrawals on Thursday, losing $199.87 million just one day after attracting $236.7 million and leading the industry's short-lived recovery.

Grayscale's GBTC recorded another $49.9 million in outflows, while Franklin Templeton's EZBC and VanEck's HODL lost $32.92 million and $23.64 million, respectively. None of the 12 Bitcoin funds recorded positive net flows during Thursday's session.

Six funds with nonzero daily net flows; six more reported zero and are omitted for readability. Two-decimal values are user-supplied and add to -$430.23M; Farside displays one-decimal amounts totaling -$430.2M. Negative means net outflows.

Source: Farside Bitcoin ETF flows, Nov. 5, 2026

The withdrawals left Bitcoin ETFs with $869.8 million in net outflows for November, extending the pressure that had already driven substantial redemptions in late October.

Man United's secret plot to poach Sir Alex Ferguson from Aberdeen, 40 years on - from phone calls with a fake Scottish accent to clandestine meetings at M74 services on Bonfire Night

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published: 04:53 EDT, 6 November 2026 | Updated: 08:07 EDT, 6 November 2026

It is 40 years ago today since Manchester United appointed Sir Alex Ferguson as manager and ushered in the most successful era in the history of the club.

The parallels with the current situation at Old Trafford are striking. United have made their worst start to a league season since then, with just one win from their opening nine games, and Michael Carrick is fighting to save his job, just as Ron Atkinson was on the brink in November 1986.

United had rejected Big Ron’s offer to resign towards the end of the previous season. However, with the team only staying out of the relegation zone on goal difference, the club decided to sack him following a 4-1 League Cup defeat by Southampton.

Atkinson felt the seeds of a decision that would change the course of United’s history had been sown a few months earlier when he was working as a TV pundit at the World Cup in Mexico and Aberdeen boss Ferguson was in temporary charge of Scotland, following the sudden death of Jock Stein.

‘Lawrie McMenemy, also on the World Cup beat as a TV pundit, revealed he had heard Alex Ferguson had already been tapped up for my job,’ Atkinson later wrote in his autobiography.

‘I haven’t lost any sleep about the shenanigans surrounding my premature departure. I don’t blame Alex one bit. It’s all part of the management game. Somebody is inevitably waiting to nick your boots in this business.’

It is 40 years since Manchester United secretively lured Sir Alex Ferguson from Aberdeen

Club director Mike Edelson put on a fake Scottish accent to get through to Ferguson's office at Aberdeen, posing as Gordon Strachan's assistant

Atkinson’s suspicions were founded on a conversation between Ferguson and United director Sir Bobby Charlton in Mexico. Ferguson confirmed the chat, but disputes its significance.

‘That’s not true,’ Ferguson wrote, addressing Atkinson’s claim in his book Managing My Life. ‘Although Bobby did speak to me at the side of the pitch before Scotland’s fiasco of a game in Uruguay, the furthest he went was to ask that if I ever decided to move to England, I should let him know. I don’t think that could be considered an offer.

‘Apart from the brief contact with Bobby, there was nothing but rumour to link me with Old Trafford before November 5.’

Charlton’s recollection also suggests it was incidental. ‘I was working for the BBC and I went to watch Scotland train,’ he told Daily Mail Sport in 2011. ‘He saw me through the fence and came over to say hello. We only spoke for a minute or so, but I liked him.

‘I also recall going to Aberdeen once, to open a garage of all things. The people there were panicking. “You’re not here to take our manager, are you?” The whole city seemed to be in love with him. I stored that away, too.’

Atkinson viewed Charlton as an adversary in the boardroom. When he returned from Mexico keen to complete the signing of England centre back Terry Butcher from Ipswich, using money earmarked from the £2.3million sale of Mark Hughes to Barcelona, Charlton was the main opponent to the move.

Chairman Martin Edwards pulled the plug citing a lack of funds due, in part, to building a new museum at Old Trafford. ‘We’ll bring the museum up for a corner then!’ Atkinson replied.

Having decided a change of manager was needed, United favoured Ferguson over Terry Venables, but were anxious to make sure the 44-year-old wanted the job having drawn a blank with McMenemy, Bobby Robson and Ron Saunders before appointing Atkinson five years earlier.

Club director Martin Edwards spoke with Fergson and met him with a secret delegation at M74 services in Lanarkshire

Ferguson pictured with Graham Hogg (right) in his first week of training at Man United

Edwards, Charlton and fellow directors Mike Edelson and Maurice Watkins hatched a bold plan to get through to Ferguson’s office at Aberdeen by posing as Gordon Strachan’s accountant.

Edelson, who put on a Scottish accent to make the call, said: ‘It suddenly got very embarrassing when the receptionist started asking me personal questions about how my wife was. The other three were rolling around. But she put me through, Alex answered, I quickly explained who I really was and then put him on to Martin.’

Ferguson recalled taking the call in his office. ‘Martin Edwards gave me his number so I could ring him back and thus be assured of the authenticity of the call. I could scarcely contain myself as I dialled the Manchester number. Would I be interested in the job at Man United? Yes, I told him without hesitation.’

Ferguson immediately told his No 2 Archie Knox, but admitted that when he told his wife Cathy she was ‘devastated’ because the family were happy in Aberdeen.

It was her reluctance to move to London that had previously persuaded him to reject Arsenal and Tottenham but this time the opportunity was too good to refuse.

Ferguson arranged to meet the four-man delegation from United at 7pm that night at Hamilton Services on the M74 in Lanarkshire, where they would recognise each other using their car registrations. ‘It really was a case of flashing headlights,’ said Edelson.

Edwards got into Ferguson’s car and the others followed to the home of Cathy’s sister Bridget in the Bishopbriggs suburb of Glasgow.

Ferguson revealed he was offered a ‘disappointing’ salary that was less than he earned in Scotland and had no transfer budget. United also refused his request to buy the family home to ensure a quick sale, or pay back a £40,000 loan from Aberdeen, but he accepted the job anyway.

Ferguson surrounded by photographers in his first game in charge - they lost 2-0 against Oxford United at the Manor Ground

He became the most successful manager in English football history, winning 13 Premier League titles

United’s directors drove back to Manchester that night and Edwards sacked Atkinson at 10.30am on the morning of November 6. Edwards then caught a 2pm flight to Aberdeen with Watkins, who was also United’s solicitor, to conclude the deal with Aberdeen chairman Dick Donald.

Meanwhile, Atkinson was clearing his desk and saying his goodbyes at United before organising a farewell drinks party at his home in Rochdale which was attended by a number of United stars and went on a lot longer than planned.

‘It turned into an all-night rave with more than a hundred people packed into the house and lasted until 5am,’ Atkinson recalled.

There were a few sore heads when a nervous Ferguson gathered the players together on November 7. He was well aware of the drinking culture at Old Trafford and what he felt was Atkinson’s liberal attitude, based on information fed to him by Strachan, who had left Aberdeen for United two years earlier.

‘He cited all sorts of problems with the team, particularly the drinking and the alleged indifference of Big Ron to its damaging effects,’ Ferguson wrote.

‘The training, Gordon suggested, was a shambles, with nothing done until Ron had a session on his sunbed as a preliminary to joining in a small-sided practice game.’

Clamping down on the boozing was one of the first items on Ferguson’s agenda, but not before he banished bus driver Derek Sutton from sitting on the bench following his first game at Oxford 48 hours after being appointed.

It ended with a 2-0 defeat followed by a goalless draw at Norwich as Ferguson made an inauspicious start to a glittering career spanning 26-and-a-half years. His first game at Old Trafford brought a first win, beating QPR 1-0.

In a new book, Fergie: The Lost Tapes, author Wayne Barton has gone through 400 hours of recordings and unearthed a long-forgotten address by the new manager to United fans that day which has echoes of the farewell speech he made on the pitch in 2013.

‘Hello, Alex Ferguson here. I don’t need to tell you how important this day is to me. This is the greatest experience of my life, to be in front of this platform and this stage.

‘Since I’ve arrived here, I’ve been so aware of the traditions and success of this great club. My job is to make sure that success continues and to make us the greatest club in Britain.

‘The real supporters of Manchester United are the greatest in the world - don’t let anyone forget it.’

An 18th-Century Connecticut Estate With ‘Little Women’ Ties Lists for $1.5 Million—Hidden Treasures Included

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

An 18th-century antique home in Wolcott, CT, with ties to a famous clockmaker and the Trans-Siberian Orchestra just hit the market for $1.5 million.

Listed for the first time in decades, the 1.94-acre property known as Spindle Hill includes a 5,360-square-foot primary residence, a hand-hewn barn, a manor home, an a history of treasure-filled walls.

"When you walk into the original section of the home, you feel the history," says listing agent Donna Membrino of William Pitt Sotheby’s International Realty. "The keeping room has an original beehive oven and a wooden mantel."

The property’s origins date to the early 1700s, when the land was purchased by the town’s first settler, the patriarch of the Alcott family, according to Membrino. Nathaniel Lane and Millicent Alcott Lane, the uncle and aunt of "Little Women" author Louisa May Alcott, later developed it into a working farm that stayed in the Lane family for generations.

The property is also linked to famed clockmaker Seth Thomas, who launched his first clockmaking venture from his childhood home just down the road.

“It was very important to the seller to preserve the home’s history, and they have taken great pains to do that,” Membrino says.

Marlene Tosun, who grew up on Spindle Hill Farm, became the owner of the property in the 1950s.

After moving in, she uncovered a series of hidden treasures: a violin beneath the floorboards of the main house; Nathaniel Lane’s diary, containing his handwritten business accounts, tucked behind a wall; and tomahawk heads embedded in the estate’s original hand-stacked stone walls, which are believed to have been built by Native Americans.

Membrino said the family also discovered a pair of 1700s wheels from Lane’s ox-drawn wagon, which was used to deliver goods to early New England customers.

The seller, Marlene’s daughter Sylvia Tosun, was once the lead vocalist for the Trans-Siberian Orchestra. She also featured on an international EDM hit championed by DJ Armin van Buuren.

In a meticulous restoration, Tosun set out to preserve the property’s original charm and character while bringing it up to modern luxury standards.

Original weathered wood salvaged from older, dismantled sections of the house was used to detail the Grand Salon. Other highlights include a black-and-white marble foyer, a 50-foot turret with an antique Schonbek chandelier, double-height cathedral ceilings, reclaimed wide-plank heart pine floors, a handcarved Italian marble chimneypiece, and three wood-burning fireplaces.

A modernized kitchen with Shaker cabinetry and granite countertops flows into a sunny dining room. Upstairs, the primary suite has a private terrace. The walk-out garden level features a wet bar and a “grand fireplace lounge” that serves as a professional recording studio.

The property also offers twin gazebos, a sunken patio, fruit trees and vegetable gardens, as well as “rare legal multi-family zoning” that allows for three private dwellings.

“Wolcott is a great town,” Membrino adds. “It’s close to Waterbury and Hartford but has more of a country feel, and it’s centrally located between New York and Boston.”

Buyers should note that “the antique barn is being sold in as-is condition,” according to the listing.

Perkz deletes X account after being fired by G2 over controversial post

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Former League of Legends pro Luka ‘Perkz’ Perković has deleted his X account after being fired by G2 Esports over a controversial social media post that sparked widespread backlash.

Perkz, who had been serving as a head coach for G2, was dropped by the esports organization on November 5, just hours after publishing a lengthy statement outlining his personal beliefs on several divisive topics.

The 27-year-old has now deleted his X account entirely, although his Instagram, Twitch and YouTube accounts remain available.

On November 4, Perkz published a 2,676-word statement on X, describing it as his “first and last honest post” in a decade.

The former League of Legends star addressed several topics, including his religious beliefs, abortion, politics, and his relationship with former G2 CEO Carlos ‘ocelote’ Rodríguez.

Among the most controversial remarks was his stance on abortion, which he compared to murder.

“I cannot comprehend how people do not understand that is a murder, as life is created at conception,” he wrote. “That is one view I will die by and never change.”

Perkz also denied supporting Andrew and Tristan Tate, defended his past loyalty to Rodríguez, and insisted he was not a supporter of Russian President Vladimir Putin.

The statement quickly attracted criticism on social media, prompting G2 Esports to announce that it was terminating its relationship with Perkz.

“While the world is complex and not everyone agrees on everything, some of the statements made last night do not align with G2’s stance and values,” G2 CEO Alban Dechelotte said.

Former G2 star Perkz defends Carlos after Andrew Tate controversy

G2 Perkz hits back at “baffling” rumors of rift with Caps

G2’s Ocelote speaks out on Perkz amid Cloud9 rumors

“We acknowledge that this statement hurt many of our fans, and we want to apologize for it.”

Following his dismissal, Perkz shared a Bible verse from Luke 6:22, which begins: “Blessed are ye, when men shall hate you, and when they shall separate you from their company.”

However, by November 6, his entire X account had been deleted, removing both his original statement and subsequent response from the platform.

His Instagram account remains accessible, and he has not issued a further public statement addressing his departure from G2 or the deletion of his X account.

G2, meanwhile, has confirmed that it will focus on rebuilding its coaching staff and roster ahead of the next competitive season, with further announcements expected once free agency officially opens.

HBAR’s 88% Slide Leaves It Ahead of Most 2021 Rivals

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Home » Trendsetter News » HBAR’s 88% Slide Leaves It Ahead of Most 2021 Rivals

A larger token supply complicates any recovery, making a return to its former price a much bigger valuation hurdle.

HBAR remains roughly 88% below its record price, yet the analyst in a recent video places it closer to recovery than most of the largest cryptocurrencies from 2021. That comparison is less a sign of strength than a measure of how severely much of that market has fallen.

Using CoinMarketCap’s November 7, 2021 rankings, the analyst selected the 50 largest coins after excluding stablecoins and wrapped assets. In the video’s snapshot, 41 remained at least 80% below their respective highs, with the median decline around 97%.

“It’s not a forecast,” Cheeky Crypto says. The figures describe historical performance, not the likelihood of a recovery.

At the roughly $0.07 price cited in the video, HBAR would need to rise about eightfold to revisit its approximately $0.57 intraday peak. The analyst separates that brief high from its strongest daily close, just above $0.50 on Binance in September 2021.

The proposed recovery levels start at $0.10, about 43% above the video’s reference price. Next comes $0.25, roughly 3.6 times that price, followed by the record itself.

HBAR has already demonstrated that a substantial rally need not restore earlier buyers’ losses.

Its strongest daily close since 2021 was around $0.37 in January 2025, according to the video, still roughly a quarter below its record close despite an approximately tenfold rise from late 2022.

The bigger obstacle is supply. Cheeky Crypto says the amount of HBAR released from treasury has more than tripled since its record, making a return to $0.57 a materially different valuation proposition.

At the supply level cited, that price would imply a market capitalization near $25 billion. Matching HBAR’s historical peak market capitalization would instead require roughly $0.11 to $0.16 per token, depending on the historical supply figures used.

That valuation is not equivalent to cash that must enter the market, a distinction the analyst explicitly makes. But additional token releases could raise the valuation required to regain the old price.

For investors, the useful distinction is between recovering a token price and recovering a network valuation. HBAR’s smaller drawdown relative to many peers does not erase dilution or make its former high an inevitable destination.

Arsenal urged to avoid £64m transfer mistake after Micah Richards claim

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Arsenal should be wary of selling Viktor Gyokeres in the January transfer window, according to former Gunners striker Jeremie Aliadiere.

Gyokeres produced his best performance of the season in midweek with a goal and an assist to help the Gunners beat Slavia Prague 2-1 in the Champions League.

But it has otherwise proved to be a frustrating few months for the Swede, who has started just one Premier League match for Mikel Arteta’s side so far this season.

Gyokeres amassed a respectable 21 goals in all competitions in his maiden season after arriving from Sporting CP for £64million, but now faces an uncertain future at the Emirates having fallen beneath Kai Havertz in the pecking order.

Sign up to our daily football newsletter, pick your team and get the latest news sent directly to your inbox every morning.

With speculation likely to mount ahead of the January window, some pundits, including Micah Richards, have suggested that the Gunners should look to move Gyokeres on.

But former Arsenal striker Aliadiere believes that might be a mistake and ultimately leave Arteta short of options at the top of the pitch.

‘I think it’s all depending on the finances how much they can get for him and who to replace him with,’ Aliadiere told Metro.

‘Because I think as much as obviously he’s not in the top 11 now because Havertz is preferred number one, you don’t want to sell him, get a good price and not be able to replace him.

‘If Havertz gets injured, which obviously we’ve seen in the past – he’s had a few injuries and he can pick up injuries – you just end up with no striker at all.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

‘So I think it’s a very difficult January transfer window. Do you try to buy someone before you sell? I think when you look over the summer, selling Trossard and Martinelli before buying somebody.

‘Obviously, they go to Tzolis, but I’m not sure Tzolis was bought to be the first choice and when he is injured, you end up doing Eze on the left-hand side, and that’s not really his best position in my opinion.

‘So I don’t want it to happen with Gyokeres, thinking let’s try to sell him and recuperate a good transfer fee for him and then not replacing him.’

But even if Gyokeres remains at the club, Aliadiere can’t envisage the 28-year-old becoming the long-term solution to Arsenal’s search for a clinical striker.

‘I just feel the way Arsenal plays does not quite suit Gyokeres’ style of play,’ Aliadiere, speaking via football betting, added.

‘I’ve always been very quite patient and looking at the good stuff that he does, and I still feel that he does some amazing work. But I just do feel that the players don’t really give him the ball quick enough, which is what he needs.

‘He needs to be on 1v1. He needs to run the channel, get fed the ball quite quickly. And I feel we’re not really playing that way. We like to control the ball.

‘We like to control the tempo and just slow down the game. And I just feel Gyokeres is more of a counter-attacking player where he sees the space, he wants it quickly and then he’s on 1v1 and then he can then dominate physically.

‘But he’s rarely getting in that position, so I just find it difficult to kind of see him turning things around in a way of probably just being a rotation player.’

Official | Antonio Di Salvo names Germany U21 squad for November 2026 internationals

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Germany U21 head coach Antonio Di Salvo has named his 23-man squad for the upcoming international fixtures against Belgium and Croatia. The selection includes two first-time call-ups and five returning players as Germany begin preparations for next summer’s European U21 Championship.

Stade Reims goalkeeper Tom Ritzy Hülsmann receives his first selection for any German national team, while FC Augsburg defender Hennes Behrens earns his maiden U21 call-up after previously featuring in Jürgen Klopp’s 44-man senior squad during the September and October international window.

Behrens is joined by Celtic midfielder Mika Baur, who was also included in Klopp’s original senior selection. Dennis Seimen, Muhammed Damar, Anton Kade, and Cajetan Lenz complete the five returning players.

Six players eligible for the U21s have meanwhile been included in Klopp’s latest senior national-team squad: Tom Bischof, Said El Mala, Brajan Gruda, Finn Jeltsch, Lennart Karl, and Wael Mohya.

Injuries have also deprived Di Salvo of Ilyas Ansah, Noahkai Banks, Noah Darvich, Aljoscha Kemlein, Assan Ouedraogo, and Nelson Weiper.

Germany face Belgium in Essen on November 13 before travelling to Croatia for their final international fixture of 2026 on November 17. Both opponents have also qualified for next summer’s European Championship.

maison margiela celebrates tokyo flagship anniversary with toothpick-drawn manga

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

an abandoned electronics factory, strange signals, and a group of researchers set the scene for the factory at night, an illustrated story that explores transformation without erasing the past.

A former electronics factory, strange signals, and a group of researchers investigating an abandoned building set the scene for The Factory at Night, an original manga commissioned by Maison Margiela for the 20th anniversary of its Tokyo flagship in Ebisu. Created by Tokyo-based artist Takehito Moriizumi, the science-fiction story translates the history of the building into an illustrated narrative, with an unusual detail defining its visual identity: the manga is drawn using a toothpick. The limited-edition zine brings together illustration, architecture, fashion, and storytelling, extending the anniversary celebrations of the house.

The narrative begins when a group of electrical engineering researchers visits an abandoned electronics factory to investigate strange signals recently detected inside. As they move through the building, they encounter an environment that seems to hold traces of its former life. Machinery, exposed wiring, industrial structures, staircases, and fragments of everyday objects populate the pages. Their investigation eventually leads to a transformation that introduces something entirely new without completely erasing what existed before.

The visual language of the manga is built around detailed black-and-white illustrations. Architectural elements often dominate the compositions, with complicated structural frameworks, overlapping staircases, suspended platforms, and exposed technical infrastructure creating a dense environment around the characters. The factory feels almost like a character in its own right, its architecture providing both the atmosphere and the narrative framework for the researchers’ investigation.

Moriizumi moves between expansive architectural scenes and more intimate manga panels, introducing the people who inhabit this unusual environment. The illustrations bring fashion into the fictional world through mannequins, garments, and references to the history of the house. The decision to work with a toothpick adds an unexpected material dimension to the publication. At a time when digital illustration, generative imagery, and increasingly automated creative processes are becoming part of visual production, the choice of an unconventional drawing tool invites a closer look at the act of making.

The Maison Margiela Tokyo store occupies a former electrical control equipment manufacturing factory at 2-8-13 Ebisu Minami, a building the house took over in 2006. Its history began six years earlier, when Martin Margiela selected a 1940s traditional Japanese residence in the same neighborhood for the brand’s first-ever retail store. The original boutique opened in September 2000, in an unexpected location away from Tokyo’s established luxury shopping districts of Ginza and Omotesando. The choice reflected the designer’s longstanding relationship with Japan, a country he and the Antwerp Six had regularly visited throughout the 1980s and 1990s.

The house was transformed through one of the Maison’s most recognizable design gestures: Bianchetto, the practice of painting objects and surfaces white. Furniture, architectural elements, and even the property’s tree stumps were covered in white paint, while the original bathroom and shared spaces were retained. When the store had to relocate in 2006, Maison Margiela chose another unconventional building just a five-minute walk away. The former factory offered a different setting, but the approach remained consistent, with the house incorporating elements of its industrial history into the new retail environment rather than erasing its previous function.

The original industrial goods elevator became fitting rooms, a shipping container housed the men’s collection, and a corridor leading to the shoes was constructed entirely from used shoeboxes. Furniture, doors, and display cases salvaged from flea markets and shops across Europe were brought to Tokyo. Parts of the original residential boutique, including its old family bathroom, were also reconstructed inside the new location, creating a physical connection between the two buildings.

Published as part of MaisonMargiela/folders, the manga forms one part of a broader initiative documenting the house’s history, creative identity, and relationship with its surroundings. The first volume of The Factory at Night is released on November 5, 2026, as a limited-edition zine available at selected newsstands across Ebisu and at the Maison Margiela Tokyo store. A digital edition is also being made available internationally through Dropbox.

Opinion: STAT+: What it’s like to live with cognitively unimpaired Alzheimer’s disease

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Karlawish is a professor of medicine, medical ethics, health policy, and neurology at the University of Pennsylvania’s Perelman School of Medicine, co-director of the Penn Memory Center, and executive producer of the “Age of Aging” podcast. He writes Neurotransmissions, a STAT column about the new era of dementia.

What is it like to have asymptomatic Alzheimer’s disease?

The answer’s obvious, isn’t it? It is like nothing. It’s a silly question, really. How could living without symptoms — the vexing troubles with concentration, word finding, recalling new facts, the loss of a mental map — be an experience of something?

Like most short and simple answers, particularly when the subject is Alzheimer’s, the obvious answer’s wrong.

Most people haven’t heard of kratom. What is it, and who’s using it?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Theresa covers substance use and addiction and co-writes the Morning Rounds newsletter. You can reach Theresa on Signal at theresagaff.97.

Over the last few years, the kratom plant and supplements made from it have garnered increasing attention from public health leaders. This broad category of products, which largely skirts any regulation, has seen major surges in potency, while becoming omnipresent on smoke shop and gas station shelves. Health officials have warned that concentrated compounds like 7-hydroxymitragynine (7-OH) are an emerging public health threat, and federal policies to limit their availability are pending. In the meantime, multiple states and smaller localities have issued emergency bans on 7-OH, and in some cases, on all kratom products.

Despite the messaging and rising use, few Americans are familiar with kratom or the newer derivative products. They’re made with compounds that have scientific names like 7-OH, mitragynine pseudoindoxyl (MGPI) and dihydro-7-hydroxy mitragynine (MGM-15) but are often sold on shelves with names like Dozo Perks, Roxy, and UltraOhmz Pseudo Supreme. New survey data from the Annenberg Public Policy Center show about two-thirds (66%) of Americans have never heard of kratom. Among those who had at least some familiarity, few understood the difference between its natural leaf form and more synthetic products.

That confusion carries over to the associated risks. In the survey of nearly 2,000 people, 66% were unsure if kratom products posed a serious public safety hazard. And most people didn’t understand health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s stance on regulation — a question that’s become complicated by repeated reports of kratom industry lobbying influence on the DEA’s proposed action.

STAT spoke with researchers and people who use or have used kratom and 7-OH to get a full picture of what these products are, along with their potential risks and benefits.

To an untrained eye, a kratom leaf might resemble a bay leaf. It grows on evergreen trees indigenous to Southeast Asian countries, including Thailand, Malaysia, and Indonesia. For centuries, people there have chewed the leaf or brewed it into tea for its stimulant-like effects.

Kratom (pronounce it with a hard or soft “a,” your choice) contains dozens of alkaloids, but mitragynine is the most abundant and the best understood. It acts as a partial agonist that binds to mu-opioid receptors in the nervous system. (Partial agonists produce less of a response from the receptor than full agonists, such as fentanyl or oxycodone.)

The first kratom products to arrive in the U.S. were made by drying and grinding up those leaves to create a matcha-like powder often used as a tea or swallowed in a capsule. In low quantities, it acts as a mild stimulant that can appear similar to coffee. In higher doses, people have used kratom to stave off chronic pain and even to help ease withdrawal from opioids. (There are no evidence-based dose recommendations, and there’s a lot of variation in both product potency and personal preference.)

“We call it swamp water, so that should give you a good visual,” said Madalyn Sklar, who uses kratom to ease chronic pain caused by degenerative disc disease, and hosts two podcasts on kratom. The first time Sklar drank the bitter tea made from kratom powder, she added orange juice as a sweetener, but still “it was so gross that I almost puked.” She’s taken it nearly every day since 2019, and says the results are better than when she was on meds like OxyContin and gabapentin.

She takes two teaspoons of powder — which works out to about 70-75 milligrams of mitragynine — every six hours. For her, there’s no high or euphoria. But she can do simple tasks that she couldn’t before, such as walk her dog and bend down to pick up its poop.

Sklar hasn’t experienced withdrawal symptoms when she’s occasionally stopped taking kratom and returned to more traditional pain medication. Research is limited, but has found that up to around 38% of kratom users may develop use disorders. In studies, withdrawal from kratom has appeared mild on a scale that measures clinical opioid withdrawal, with symptoms like anxiety, fatigue, gastrointestinal issues, and restlessness.

Before semi-synthetic products came onto the scene, most public health concern around kratom was directed at extracts and tinctures, recalled Sklar. Many of these products still tout mitragynine as the active ingredient, but in much more concentrated volumes than the whole leaf powder — think 100 to 300 milligrams of mitragynine in a bottle of liquid the size of a 5-hour Energy. (There are currently no federal proposals that aim to regulate concentrated mitragynine products.)

“But people got really interested in the other alkaloids over time,” said Katherine Hill, an epidemiologist at the Yale School of Public Health who has studied kratom. “And specifically they got interested in 7-hydroxymitrogynine.”

The alkaloid 7-OH does not appear in the fresh kratom leaf that people in Southeast Asia chew, according to Hill. It shows up in the dried leaf powder products, in small amounts. It’s also a metabolite, meaning that when the body absorbs mitragynine, some of it turns it into 7-OH. Once inside the body, 7-OH, like mitragynine, is a partial agonist to the mu-opioid receptor.

The products made with 7-OH — including tablets, gummies, shots, and drink powders — are semi-synthetic; they contain artificially concentrated amounts of the compound. When the products first started popping up, they were often sold in tablets containing 12-14 milligrams, users said. Those can offer sedative effects and pain relief. But now, tablets containing 25, 50, and even 100 milligrams of 7-OH are available.

“The first time I heard of 7-OH, it was very negative,” said Twinkle VanFleet, who co-hosts a kratom podcast with Sklar.

While natural kratom products carry a risk of dependence and what’s thought to be mild withdrawal at low doses, there’s growing concern that concentrated 7-OH products are even more addictive, and that withdrawal can be as bad as coming off a traditional opioid. Hill is working on a study of 7-OH users that will look at both tolerance and withdrawal. But for now, the risks are mainly documented in case reports, series, and other anecdotal accounts.

VanFleet suffers from intense pain from an injury that led to complex regional pain syndrome, and she has a deep mistrust of the medical system. Kratom didn’t ease her symptoms, so she eventually tried 7-OH, and found comfort in what she calls “nibbles” of smaller tablets. She currently takes around 12 milligrams per day and worries about a ban by the Drug Enforcement Administration. Still, she shares concerns about the availability of higher-dose products.

“I do see both sides of a lot of this,” VanFleet said. “In my perfect world, there would be 3, 5, and 10 milligram tablets, period. If even that.”

Greg Kiesow had been sober from alcohol for nearly 20 years and retired for three days when he went into a smoke shop that was promoting 7-OH products. The shopkeeper had heard it was some sort of natural energy supplement, like coffee, and gave Kiesow a free sample of tablets. “I took it home, and I took it. And 10 to 15 minutes later, it wasn’t coffee. It was a very powerful opioid,” Kiesow said.

Kiesow said he quickly developed an addiction. After 10 months, which he described as an extremely isolated period spent sitting on his back porch watching the birds at his bird feeder, he began to taper himself off the products. A few weeks later, he formed Kratom Anonymous, which has 15 virtual meetings per week for people looking to quit.

As 7-OH gained popularity, newer synthetic products have continued to crop up, including some with concentrated levels of mitragynine pseudoindoxyl (MGPI), another metabolite of mitragynine. Over the summer, the DEA proposed a ban of MGPI at any quantity. But an updated proposal in October, pushed by a lobbyist for the American Kratom Association, would allow small amounts of the compound to show up in products. Instead of an outright ban, the DEA now proposes limiting both MGPI and 7-OH based on the ratio of those compounds to mitragynine in any given product.

The reason for the change, according to the DEA, is that trace amounts of MGPI could appear in natural kratom. But the safety of small amounts is unclear. Test results presented in recent wrongful death lawsuits suggest that a natural kratom powder product, used by somebody who died after taking it, was likely contaminated with MGPI.

As more people use kratom and other associated products, there’s a growing recognition of the potential risk for addiction. Over 10 years, the National Poison Data Center found a 1,200% increase in kratom-related exposure reports — from 258 in 2015 to 3,434 in 2025. That includes all types of products. Clinicians who run addiction treatment centers say, over the last year, they’ve seen a rise in patients seeking help to get off 7-OH in particular.

But data on the most serious risks — overdose and death — are murky. The poison center data showed 233 deaths over the decade, most of which involved other substances like opioids, benzodiazepines, stimulants, and alcohol. (Researchers believe kratom can exacerbate the danger of a drug like fentanyl, and even more common medications like certain antidepressants.) A 2023 investigation by the Tampa Bay Times found that, over nearly 10 years, more than 580 people in Florida alone died from overdoses that involved kratom products.

Because both 7-OH and MGPI are metabolites of mitragynine, it can be difficult to draw conclusions about a given death, even with toxicology data. Complicating the matter even further: When tested, many kratom products — natural and synthetic — are found to have different concentration levels than the labels advertise, and some have been found to contain compounds that were not on the label at all.

Sklar and VanFleet are both proponents of regulations, not bans, on all types of kratom-related products. Others who advocate for natural kratom are working hard to distance themselves from the more synthetic products.

“Any who unwittingly support every concentrated 7-OH product are not defending the natural kratom plant. It may be helping to destroy it,” Mac Haddow, the American Kratom Association’s lobbyist, said in a recent video. “The solution is not to defend everything. The solution is to draw a clear, bright line.”

But others said they’ve suffered from taking natural kratom products, too. Danelle Kawasaki first tried kratom at a dry bar in her Montana town. It came at room temperature, and was advertised as a natural, herbal tea. The concoction eased her anxiety, making her feel both calm and energetic, able to speak with anyone she wanted. At first it was a weekly treat, then a few times a week. After a few months, she was having two drinks per day. She’d wake up with a jolt of anxiety, which she attributes to the tea not being in her system overnight. Without more, she’d be sick, crawling to the bathroom to puke.

It frustrates Kawasaki when people try to tell her that 7-OH, not natural kratom, is the problem. “This argument comes up all the time and it drives me nuts,” she said. “I know what was going on — it was the actual kratom tea powder. It wasn’t a concentrated form.”

At one point, she was drinking nearly 140 ounces of the tea per day. When she finally stopped, cold turkey, the withdrawal was its own dayslong nightmare.

Now, she advocates that the Fort Peck tribe, of which she’s a member, ban kratom on the reservation. For Kawasaki, “the risks outweigh the remedy that people are searching for.”

STAT’s coverage of chronic health issues is supported by a grant from Bloomberg Philanthropies. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Opinion: AI depends on medical records that don’t always tell the full truth about women

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

By Annemarie G. Hirsch and Jennifer Silva

Hirsch and Silva are authors of “Seen But Not Heard: What Medical Records Don’t Tell Us About Women’s Lives.”

In September, the Gallup-Pivotal Women’s Health Survey reported that 34% of U.S. women strongly or somewhat agreed that a health care provider minimized or dismissed their symptoms in the past five years. Thirty-three percent reported being misdiagnosed or unable to get a diagnosis, compared with 21% of men reporting these experiences.

Medical records tell a different story. An epidemiologist and a sociologist, we recently released a study where we paired electronic medical records with detailed interviews with 87 women. We talked to these women about their pain and illnesses, work, family, poverty, violence, and encounters with the health care system.

Fifty-three of the women described a trust-breaking encounter with a health system, but only seven of them had medical records that documented mistrust. Thirty-two women reported experiencing a misdiagnosis, but misdiagnosis was only documented for eight of these women. Thirty women reported that they felt that clinicians treated them with disrespect, but this was not documented in the records anywhere.

For more than a decade, Kate lived with debilitating pelvic pain. Her doctors repeatedly attributed her symptoms to obesity. Her medical record reflected these assumptions.

When Kate underwent bariatric surgery and lost a significant amount of weight, her clinical notes became optimistic.

However, her symptoms persisted. Medical records pivoted from linking symptoms from obesity to mental health factors.

“Gastrointestinal malfunctions arising from mental factors.”

The turning point came only after Kate attended an appointment with her new husband. In the clinical notes, the provider documented Kate’s husband’s concerns:

For the first time in 10 years, doctors conducted the exams needed to finally diagnose Kate with endometriosis.

Kate’s medical record accurately documented years of conversations about weight and mental health. What it failed to capture was equally important: the cost of years spent with an undiagnosed disease, the frustration of not being believed, and the erosion of trust that occurs when symptoms are repeatedly explained away rather than investigated. Kate told us, “If I was tired, it was because I was overweight. If I had cramps, it was because I was overweight. Migraines, I was overweight. … They’re not hearing what you’re saying, and that compounded the issues that were going on.”

We often think of medical records as objective accounts of a patient’s health. They are not. Medical records are narratives, shaped by the questions clinicians ask and the assumptions they bring into the room. Medical record data reflect clinical decisions as well as biology. The language clinicians use and the details they choose to record can reinforce stereotypes that follow patients from one encounter to the next, influencing how future clinicians interpret symptoms, assess credibility, and make treatment decisions.

Medical record data also reflect what patients feel comfortable disclosing. What women share with their doctors depends on how much they trust them and how safe they feel. When people fear judgment, dismissal, or not being believed, important parts of their stories may remain unspoken, and thus, undocumented.

This fear may why 53 women in our data described a trust-breaking clinical encounter in their interview, while only seven of their medical records noted their mistrust. This is particularly a problem for women, who feel the pressure to be pleasant and pliant with doctors for fear of not being taken seriously.

Across the country, clinics and hospitals are adopting AI scribes that transcribe recorded interactions between doctor and patient and produce a clinical note. AI tools can be used to search and synthesize medical records to help with diagnostics and treatment plans, some with great success.

However, these tools are only as good as the data in the medical record. If a woman’s symptoms are filtered through assumptions about her weight or mental health, and the true story of her pain is omitted, AI may learn and reproduce how the woman is framed in the record.

For AI to achieve its full potential in women’s health, medical records need to tell the complete story. Health care settings must be safe spaces where women can tell their stories; clinicians need to listen to women, even when their experiences do not fit clinical expectations; and clinicians need to document in the medical record when diagnoses are delayed or incorrect, without fear of being penalized.

Armed with these data, AI has the potential to do more than replicate biases of the past. AI could help identify patterns of missed diagnoses, reduce disparities in care, and ensure that more women are heard.

Annemarie G. Hirsch, Ph.D., M.P.H., and Jennifer Silva, Ph.D., are authors of “Seen But Not Heard: What Medical Records Don’t Tell Us About Women’s Lives.”

STAT+: After Medicare punts RPM overhaul to 2029, advocates see window to fight back

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mario covers technology in health care, including FDA regulation of artificial intelligence; how Medicare pays for health tech; the use of AI in clinical care; mental health chatbots; and consumer wearables. He’s also the co-author of the free, twice weekly STAT Health Tech newsletter. You can reach Mario on Signal at mariojoze.13.

Medicare regulators this week pushed forward with a controversial plan to ban hospitals and other providers from using third parties to deliver remote patient monitoring services, despite overwhelming opposition from health tech companies, hospitals, insurers, and specialty organizations.

But regulators also offered providers a reprieve in the form of a two-year delay in implementation that’s aimed at allowing time for providers to build in-house services. Advocates representing vendors, hospitals, and other stakeholders see that window as an opportunity to push for another plan.

“The story is that we have two years to come up with something better,” said Christopher Adamec, a lobbyist who advises the Remote Monitoring Leadership Council, a group of leading vendors. “My mission here is to find and move forward a value-based payment pathway or some set of guardrails, or some alternative to this.”

Guy Roux, it’s over

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In four and a half decades of (almost) absolute loyalty at the head of AJ Auxerre, Guy Roux has set almost unbreakable longevity records in the landscape of French football. The iconic coach with the hat has notably etched his name in history by directing 894 first division matches (including four during his very short stint in Lens), an absolute peak in Ligue 1 which testifies to a bygone era when projects were long-term.

This statistical prowess is not limited to the national championship, since the child from Colmar has long stood alone on another summit: that of European victories acquired at the head of a French club. A historic record that modern football and its binge on matches are about to bring down

It is precisely this record of victories on the continental scene which is today in the process of escaping him. By winning on the pitch at Bayer Leverkusen with Olympique de Marseille, Bruno Genesio achieved the 41st success of his career in the European Cup, in all competitions, with a French club. A major performance which allows him to match the legendary mark of the former Auxerre coach.

Having increased his record from OL to LOSC, then from Rennes to OM, Bruno Genesio is preparing to overtake Guy Roux, but could quickly have to lose to Luis Enrique, who already has 31 victories at the head of PSG in just over two seasons.

If Bruno Genesio has to his credit the semi-final of the Champions League played with Olympique Lyonnais in 2020 or the direct qualification for the round of 16 with Losc, two years ago, Guy Roux’s continental achievements with a club with the profile of Auxerre are a romantic feat.

With incomparable financial means, the Burgundian technician took AJA to the semi-final of the UEFA Cup in 1993 – cruelly eliminated on penalties by Borussia Dortmund – before inviting himself among the eight best teams on the continent in the quarter-final of the Champions League in 1997, defeating European giants on the Abbé-Deschamps lawn.

During his long successive leases at AS Monaco and especially on the Arsenal bench, the Alsatian technician totaled more than 120 victories on the European scene (including the Champions League and the various C3s), placing him irremediably out of reach of most French technicians.
